The families analytic index for 1 | 1 1|1 -dimensional Euclidean field theories
Daniel Berwick-Evans

Abstract
. We construct a KO-valued families index for a class of 1 | 1 1|1 -dimensional Euclidean field theories. This realizes a conjectured cocycle map in the Stolz–Teichner program. We further show that a bundle of spin manifolds leads to a family of partially-defined 1 | 1 1|1 -Euclidean field theories, yielding a cocycle refinement of the families analytic index. The methods are chosen with the goal of generalizing to 2 | 1 2|1 -dimensional field theories, where analogous structures are expected to provide an analytic index valued in topological modular forms.



1.
Introduction and statement of results

Stolz and Teichner have outlined constructions of categories of
1|1
- and
2|1
-dimensional Euclidean field theories over a smooth manifold
M
indexed by a degree
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}

[
ST04
,
HST10
,
ST11
]
.
They conjecture that these categories determine geometric cocycle models for real
{\rm K}
-theory and topological modular forms
[
ST11
, §1.5-1.6]
,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}d|1\hbox{-}{\rm dimensional}\\
{\rm Euclidean\ field\ theories}\\
{\rm of\ degree}\ n\ {\rm over}\ M\end{array}\right\}
\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\rm KO}^{n}(M)&d=1\\
{\rm TMF}^{n}(M)&d=2.\end{array}\right.
cocycle

It is further expected that these cocycle models inherit an analytic pushforward from quantization of field theories. The ultimate goal is to use these pushforwards to prove a TMF-generalization of the Atiyah–Singer index theorem.

The feeling has always been that a proper understanding of the relationship between
1|1
-Euclidean field theories and the index theorem in K-theory will provide significant insight into the
2|1
-dimensional case
[
Seg88
, §6]
,
[
ST04
, §3]
. With this in mind, the goal of this paper is to formulate and prove a version of (
1
) when
d=1
that communicates with the families analytic index. We use a variation on the notion of degree
n
field theories over
M
, denoted
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
, that incorporates reflection positivity and reality data as well as a condition demanding the existence of smooth cutoff theories; see §
1.2
below.

Theorem 1.1
.

An object of
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
determines a self-adjoint, Clifford linear superconnection
{\mathbb{A}}
on a real vector bundle
V\to M
(not necessarily finite-rank). The families index construction for self-adjoint superconnections determines a cocycle map

(7)

\displaystyle 1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)\xrightarrow{{\rm Index}}\widehat{\rm KO}{}^{n}(M)

valued in the differential
{\rm KO}
-theory of
M
. If the renormalization group flow sends an object
E\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
to the zero theory, then its underlying class in
{\rm KO}^{n}(M)
is zero.

The categories
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
also accommodate a refinement of the KO-valued families analytic index for a bundle of spin manifolds.

Theorem 1.2
.

The Bismut superconnection of a family of Riemannian spin manifolds
X\to M
determines an object
\alpha(X)\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{-n}(M)
whose image under (
7
) is the families differential analytic index of
X\to M
.

With the knowledge that the existing definitions of supersymmetric Euclidean field theory are unlikely to be the final word (see Remark
B.1
and
B.11
), the cocycle map (
7
) is designed to be as robust as possible.
Indeed, the categories
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
and Theorem
1.1
come from evaluating a field theory on specific families of
1|1
-Euclidean bordisms, and the structures relevant to index theory are inherited from the geometry of these bordisms. The upshot is that any reasonable category of
1|1
-Euclidean field theories over
M
has a subcategory with a cocycle map (
7
); see Remark
1.13
. Perhaps more importantly, the categories
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
have a generalization to the
d=2
version of the conjecture (
1
), and the techniques behind Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
lay the groundwork for this
d=2
sequel
[
BE23c
]
.

1.1.
The main idea

In the physics literature, a
1|1
-dimensional field theory is more commonly called
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetric quantum mechanics.
The basic datum is a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded inner product space
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\rm ev}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{\rm odd}
and an odd operator
Q\colon\mathcal{H}^{{\rm ev}/{\rm odd}}\to\mathcal{H}^{{\rm odd}/{\rm ev}}

[
Wit82
, §1]
. This determines a representation of the super semigroup

(8)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}\to{\rm End}(\mathcal{H}),\qquad(t,\theta)\mapsto e^{-tQ^{2}+\theta Q},

that enhances the usual time-evolution semigroup in Wick-rotated quantum mechanics. Depending on the type of theory desired, one demands additional structure and property on (
8
). For example,
compact
theories require that (
8
) takes values in trace-class operators, and
reflection-positive
theories require
Q
to be self-adjoint. The main example motivating (
1
) takes
Q=\not{D}
to be the Dirac operator for a compact spin manifold
X

[
ST04
, Example 3.2.9]
. This theory is both compact and reflection positive.

The super semigroup
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}
has a geometric interpretation as a moduli space of super intervals
[
ST04
, §3]
for which multiplication in the super semigroup corresponds to gluing of super intervals. Super intervals comprise a subcategory of the
1|1
-Euclidean bordism category
[
HST10
, §6]
,

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm super}\\
{\rm intervals}\end{array}\!\!\right\}\xhookrightarrow{\iota}\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}1|1\hbox{-}{\rm Euclidean}\\
{\rm bordisms}\end{array}\!\!\right\}\implies\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}1|1\hbox{-}{\rm Euclidean}\\
{\rm field\ theories}\end{array}\!\!\right\}\xrightarrow{\iota^{*}}{\rm Rep}({\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0})

and
1|1
-Euclidean field theories are defined as representations of this bordism category. In particular, a functor out of the
1|1
-Euclidean bordism category restricts along
\iota
to a representation of
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}
. The representations (
8
) that come from this restriction have additional properties, e.g., compactness is automatic
[
ST12
, §2]
. The initial definition of
1|1
-Euclidean field theory from
[
ST04
, §3]
simply equips representations of
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}
with these expected additional properties. The more recent definition from
[
ST11
]
instead gives a precise notion of the
1|1
-Euclidean bordism category, and defines a
1|1
-Euclidean field theory as a representation of this bordism category. One upshot of the newer definition is a natural generalization to field theories over a smooth manifold, gotten by equipping
1|1
-Euclidean bordisms with a map to a (background) manifold
M
, see Definition
1.6
below.

Super intervals with a map to
M
determine the category of
superpaths
in
M
. This leads to a families-generalization of (
1.1
)

\left\{\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm super\ paths}\\
{\rm in}\ M\end{array}\!\!\!\right\}\xhookrightarrow{\iota}\left\{\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}1|1\hbox{-}{\rm Euclidean}\\
{\rm bordisms\ over}\ M\end{array}\!\!\!\right\}\implies\left\{\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}1|1\hbox{-}{\rm Euclidean\ field}\\
{\rm theories\ over}\ M\end{array}\!\!\!\right\}\xrightarrow{\iota^{*}}{\rm Rep}\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm super\ paths}\\
{\rm in}\ M\end{array}\!\!\!\right),

where a functor out of the
1|1
-Euclidean bordism category over
M
determines a representation of the category of superpaths in
M
by restriction. Examples of representations of the category of superpaths arise from
super parallel transport
along a superconnection
[
Dum08
]
; a folklore theorem extends these representations to
1|1
-Euclidean field theories over
M

[
ST11
, §1.3]
.

The basic mechanism behind Theorem
1.1
is an inverse to this super parallel transport construction: a
1|1
-Euclidean field theory over
M
determines a superconnection via restriction to
nearly constant superpaths
in
M
, denoted
{\sf sPath}_{0}(M)
. These are superpaths whose maps to
M
factor through the projection
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}
.

Proposition 1.3

(Proposition
6.20
)
.

There is a functor

{\rm Rep}({\sf sPath}_{0}(M))\to{\sf Vect}^{{\mathbb{A}}}(M)

from (not necessarily finite-rank) representations of
{\sf sPath}_{0}(M)
to the groupoid of (not necessarily finite-rank) super vector bundles with superconnection on
M
.

This leads to a natural candidate for the cocycle map (
1
)

\displaystyle\left\{\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}1|1\hbox{-}{\rm Euclidean\ field}\\
{\rm theories\ over}\ M\end{array}\!\!\!\right\}\xrightarrow{\iota^{*}}{\rm Rep}({\sf sPath}_{0}(M))\xrightarrow{{\rm Prop~\ref{lem:mainA}}}{\sf Vect}^{{\mathbb{A}}}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm Index}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}{\rm KO}^{0}(M)

where the last arrow is the families index construction applied to a superconnection, e.g., see
[
BGV92
, Chapter 9]
.

For an arbitrary superconnection, the index construction may not be valid—one needs certain additional properties. Some of these properties are automatic when a representation of
{\sf sPath}_{0}(M)
comes from the restriction of a
1|1
-Euclidean field theory. For example, the operators
e^{-t{\mathbb{A}}^{2}}
are self-adjoint and trace class for
t>0
. However, these conditions alone do not guarantee the existence of an index bundle. The precise conditions on the superconnection can be complicated to state and depend sensitively on the analytical framework, e.g., compare Lott and Gorokhovsky’s superconnections on Hilbert bundles
[
GL15
]
.

From the field theory point of view, the additional property required to construct (
1.1
) is quite straightforward: one needs the existence of
smooth energy cutoffs
. Roughly, this means that a
1|1
-Euclidean field theory over
M
determines a compatible
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
-family of theories that discard states of energy greater than
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
. The states with energy
<\lambda
are used to construct the families index. The existence of smooth energy cutoffs (and hence the existence of a families index) is a
property
of a field theory, not additional data. Furthermore, this property makes sense for Euclidean field theories in any super dimension, in particular dimension
2|1
. The existence of smooth cutoffs is analogous to the ellipticity property for differential operators, see Definition
1.11
and Remark
1.14
. The categories
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
are roughly defined as the value of a field a
1|1
-Euclidean field theory on nearly constant superpaths (see §
1.2
) where in additional we require a smooth cutoff property; the following provides an index-theoretic characterization.

Proposition 1.4

(Proposition
7.3
)
.

There is an equivalence of categories

(28)

\displaystyle{\rm sPar}\colon{\sf Vect}^{{\mathbb{A}}}_{\rm ind}(M)\xrightarrow{\sim}1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{0}(M)

from the category of real, self-adjoint superconnections admitting smooth index bundles to the category
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{0}(M)
.

In short,
{\sf Vect}^{{\mathbb{A}}}_{\rm ind}(M)\subset{\sf Vect}^{{\mathbb{A}}}(M)
is the subcategory of superconnections on which the (differential) index map is defined. The functor
{\rm sPar}
is totally explicit, e.g.,

(29)

\displaystyle e^{-t{\mathbb{A}}^{2}+\theta{\mathbb{A}}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}\to{\rm End}(\Omega^{\bullet}(M;V_{\mathbb{C}}))

determines the underlying representation of
{\sf sPath}_{0}(M)
under (
28
). The formula (
29
) also coincides with super parallel transport along a superconnection
{\mathbb{A}}
on a finite rank vector bundle; e.g., see
[
Dum12
, Theorem 1]
,
[
Sto19
, Proposition 6.7]
or
[
BEH16
, §2]
. This motivates the notation
{\rm sPar}
for the functor (
28
), as it provides a partial construction of super parallel transport for more general superconnections; see Remark
1.5
below. Proposition
1.4
allows us to construct the cocycle map in Theorem
1.1
as the composition (
1.1
).

Remark 1.5
.

Dumitrescu’s super parallel transport
[
Dum08
]
extends (
28
) to a functor

{\sf Vect}^{\mathbb{A}}_{{\rm fin}}(M)\to 1|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}^{0}(M),

from superconnection on finite-rank super vector bundles; compare Corollary
7.4
. An infinite-rank generalization of super parallel transport would provide a complete dictionary between
1|1
-Euclidean field theories and superconnections.
However, in our present analytical setting (following
[
ST11
]
)) super parallel transport for infinite rank bundles need not be defined owing to the failure of existence and uniqueness results for differential equations in topological vector spaces, e.g., see
[
Ham82
, Example 5.6.1]
for counterexamples in Fréchet spaces. From the physics point of view, one expects field theories to be determined by infinitesimal data.
We note that some of the prior definitions of
1|1
-Euclidean field theory leverage existence and uniqueness results in Hilbert spaces
[
HST10
, §6]
. In a different direction, there are existence and uniqueness results for tame Fréchet spaces and tame maps. Adopting a more Wilsonian approach, one could define
1|1
-Euclidean field theories as an
{\rm Ind}
-completion of finite-rank objects with compatibilities via the energy filtration; this also forces existence and uniqueness. When attempting to weigh these options, the main concern is that the correct analytical context for the
d=2
case in conjecture (
1
) is not yet clear. As such, we leave these choices of analysis flexible to avoid over-constraining the possible definitions in this
d=2
generalization.

1.2.
Technical overview

The categories
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
in Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
come from distilling the information of a degree
n
Euclidean field theory down to the essential pieces required to formulate the index map (
7
). We work in the framework for field theories developed by Stolz and Teichner in
[
ST11
]
, and begin
with a brief recollection of their main definition; see §
B
for a more detailed overview.

Definition 1.6

(
[
ST11
, §4-5]
)
.

For
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
the
degree
n
twist
is a
\otimes
-invertible functor

(30)

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}^{n}\colon 1|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}({\rm pt})\to{\sf{Alg}}

where
{\sf{Alg}}
is a Morita category of (topological) algebras, bimodules, and bimodule maps. Pulling back along the canonical functor
1|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}(M)\to 1|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}({\rm pt})
we obtain a twist for the
1|1
-Euclidean bordism category over
M
that we will also denote by
\mathscr{T}^{n}
.
A
degree
n
field theory over
M
is a natural transformation

(31)

1|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}(M)
{\sf{Alg}}.
E\Downarrow
\mathbbold{1}
\mathscr{T}^{n}

Remark 1.7
.

Some details of the above definition have not appeared in the literature; we refer to §
5
for further discussion that culminates in Hypothesis
5.9
.

Definition 1.8

(Definitions
B.30
,
B.32
, and
5.16
, assuming Hypothesis
5.9
)
.

Define a category
1|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}^{n}(M)
whose objects are real, reflection positive degree
n
field theories (
31
). Morphisms in
1|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}^{n}(M)
are isomorphisms between internal natural transformations
E\simeq E^{\prime}
that are compatible with the real and reflection positive structures.

Remark 1.9
.

Reflection positivity is perhaps unnecessary in the
d=1
version of conjecture (
1
): unoriented, real field theories (with a different positivity condition) recover KO-theory
[
HST10
]
, see Remark
5.18
. However, unoriented and real structures do not generalize to the
2|1
-dimensional Euclidean bordism category, and the Miller character
{\rm TMF}\to{\rm KO}(\!(q)\!)
necessitates a map from
2|1
-Euclidean field theories to real K-theory if conjecture (
1
) is to hold. Reflection positivity is one way to obtain real structures on Hilbert spaces of states in dimension
2|1
; see
[
GPPV21
, 3.2.2]
and
[
BE23c
]
.

In §
3
-§
4
, we construct a category
{\sf sPath}_{0}(M)
of nearly constant super Euclidean paths in
M
with an internal functor
\iota

(32)

{\sf sPath}_{0}(M)
1|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}(M)
{\sf{Alg}}.
\Downarrow
\iota
\mathbbold{1}
\mathscr{T}^{n}

When applied to degree zero theories, restriction along
\iota
makes (
1.1
) precise. Together with Proposition
1.3
, we obtain the composition (
1.1
) that extract a superconnection from a
1|1
-Euclidean field theory.

Proposition 1.10
.

The restriction of a degree
n
Euclidean field theory (
31
) along the functor (
32
) determines:

(1)

vector bundles
V_{+},V_{-}\to M
with fiberwise
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{+n}
- and
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}
-actions and Clifford linear superconnections
{\mathbb{A}}_{+}
and
{\mathbb{A}}_{-}
, respectively (Proposition
6.20
);

(2)

a pairing between section-valued differential forms (Proposition
6.26
)

(33)

\displaystyle\langle-,-\rangle\colon{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}}\Big(\Omega^{\bullet}(M;V_{-})\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}(M;V_{+})\Big)\to\Omega^{\bullet}(M)

for which the superconnections satisfy
\langle{\mathbb{A}}_{-}x,y\rangle=(-1)^{|x|}\langle x,{\mathbb{A}}_{+}y\rangle
;

(3)

differential forms
Z,Z_{\ell}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{>0}))
satisfying

dZ=0,\ \ \partial_{\ell}Z=dZ_{\ell},\qquad{\rm deg}(Z)=n\mod 2,\ \ {\rm deg}(Z_{\ell})=n-1\mod 2

where
\ell
is the standard coordinate on
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
(Proposition
6.44
).

The renormalization group flow on degree
n
field theories induces the Getzler rescaling of superconnections (Proposition
6.22
): renormalization flow by
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
restricts to

(34)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}_{\pm}\mapsto\mu({\mathbb{A}}_{\pm})_{0}+({\mathbb{A}}_{\pm})_{1}+\mu^{-1}({\mathbb{A}}_{\pm})_{2}+\mu^{-2}({\mathbb{A}}_{\pm})_{3}+\dots

Reflection and real structures restrict along (
32
) as follows.

(1)

A reflection structure determines isomorphisms
V_{\pm}\simeq\overline{V}_{\mp}
of bundles of Clifford modules relative to the
*
-superalgebra structure
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{\pm n}\to\overline{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}}_{\mp n}
defined in (
202
). Using this data, the pairing (
33
) determines graded hermitian pairings on
V_{+}
and
V_{-}
for which the superconnections
{\mathbb{A}}_{+}
and
{\mathbb{A}}_{-}
are graded self-adjoint. The positivity condition requires the hermitian pairing to be positive (Proposition
6.32
).

(2)

A real structure determines isomorphisms
V_{\pm}\simeq\overline{V}_{\pm}
of bundles of modules relative to the standard real structure on
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}
. The superconnections are compatible with this real structure on
V_{\pm}
. For a reflection positive theory with real structure, the hermitian inner product is also real (Proposition
6.37
).

As described before Proposition
1.4
, the upshot of Proposition
1.10
is that a degree
n
Euclidean field theory (
31
) with real and reflection positive structures determines a superconnection for which a
{\rm KO}^{n}
-valued families index construction
might
apply. The existence of this index bundle can be a rather subtle analytical question. The main condition one requires is that a certain direct sum of eigenspaces for eigenvalues less than a fixed
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
form a smooth vector bundle over (an open submanifold) of
M
; see Definition
C.16
. Physically, this subbundle of eigenspaces is the subspace of states with energy less than
\lambda
. Rephrasing in physical language, the existence of the index bundle is the same as demanding the existence of a smooth cutoff theory in the sense of Wilsonian effective field theory, e.g., see
[
Cos11a
, §1.3]
for a mathematical exposition of the key ideas. The following definition captures the precise pieces of a
1|1
-Euclidean field theory required to extract a KO-valued families index.

Definition 1.11
.

The groupoid
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
has objects degree
n
representations of
{\sf sPath}_{0}(M)
(Definition
6.18
) that are reflection positive (Definition
6.30
), trace class (Definition
6.41
), real (Definition
6.35
), and admit cutoffs (Definition
7.1
). Morphisms are isomorphisms between degree
n
representations of
{\sf sPath}_{0}(M)
that are compatible with the reflection structure, real structure, and trace.

Corollary 1.12
.

Theorem
1.1
constructs a cocycle map for a subcategory of Stolz and Teichner’s degree
n
theories,

\displaystyle\{E\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}^{n}(M)\mid E\ {\rm admits\ energy\ cutoffs}\}\xrightarrow{{\rm cocycle}}\widehat{\rm KO}{}^{n}(M).

Proof.

The definition of
2|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
is set up so that an object
E\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}^{n}(M)
determines the
data
of an object in
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
by restriction to specific families of super Euclidean bordisms; it remains to check that these data satisfy the required properties. The positivity property for an object in
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
follows from reflection positivity for
1|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}^{n}(M)
. Field theories satisfying the cutoff property provide a subcategory of field theories, and we have a span

(35)

\displaystyle 1|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}^{n}(M)\hookleftarrow\{E\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf EFT}}^{n}(M)\mid E\ {\rm admits\ energy\ cutoffs}\}\xrightarrow{{\rm restrict}}1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M).

Postcomposing the map on the right with (
7
) provides the claimed map to
\widehat{\rm KO}{}^{n}(M)
.
∎

Theorem
1.2
follows from turning Proposition
1.10
around: the Bismut superconnection determines a representation of
{\sf sPath}_{0}(M)
from the formula (
29
), and this representation naturally comes with the additional data and properties required to determine an object in
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
. In this example, the existence of cutoffs relies on Bismut’s families index theorem
[
Bis86
]
. Despite only being part of the data of a field theory (see Remark
1.15
),
\alpha(X)\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{-n}(M)
contains all the relevant data for the families differential index.

We conclude with several remarks about the framework developed in this paper.

Remark 1.13
.

One can formulate the span (
35
) for any definition of degree
n
,
1|1
-Euclidean field theories defined using a bordism category that contains the families of small bordisms (see Definition
4.23
) where the degree twist satisfies Hypothesis
5.9
. Hence, such field theories admit a subcategory with a cocycle map valued in
\widehat{\rm KO}{}^{n}(M)
by composing with (
7
). For finite-rank examples, the cutoff condition is automatically satisfied, see Corollary
7.4
which follows from
[
BGV92
, §9.1]
. Hence, the cutoff condition in (
35
) is only interesting for infinite-rank examples.

Remark 1.14
.

Cutoffs make sense for a broad class of field theories. Indeed, in Witten’s study of the
\mathcal{N}=(0,1)
supersymmetric sigma model
[
Wit88
, §1]
, he suggested that cutoffs be used to analyze the field theories in the
d=2
version of the conjectures (
1
).
For
1|1
-dimensional field theories, the property of having smooth energy cutoffs is analogous to the property of a differential operator being elliptic; in particular, cutoffs require eigenfunctions of the degree zero part of
{\mathbb{A}}^{2}
to determine smooth sections over
M
.

Remark 1.15
.

Assuming a generators and relations description of the category
1|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}(M)
(following
[
HST10
, 6.7]
and
[
ST11
, 3.2]
), the data of an object of
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
is the data of a degree
n
field theory defined on a subcategory of
small bordisms in
M

1|1\hbox{-{\sf ebord}}(M)\hookrightarrow 1|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}(M)

generated by the
S
-families of bordisms in Definition
4.23
.

Remark 1.16
.

By results of Hopkins–Hovey
[
HH92
]
, Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
yield a surjection

(36)

\displaystyle 1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm KO}^{n}(M),

verifying a version of the
d=1
case of (
1
).
A stronger form of the conjectures (
1
) asserts that the map (
36
) induces an isomorphism on concordance classes, so that the corresponding cohomology theories are a complete invariant of field theories
[
ST11
, §1.5]
. It is easy to see that (
36
) factors through concordance classes, but we do not expect an isomorphism in our framework used below for reasons we explain presently.
In view of the factorization (
1.1
) of the cocycle map, the stronger form of conjecture (
1
) in particular requires a category of superconnections whose concordance classes are KO-theory. But in existing definitions the isomorphism class of the underlying vector bundle is a concordance invariant. In particular, the rank of a vector bundle is a concordance invariant, but is not an invariant of the underlying
{\rm KO}
-class. We see two possible ways to avoid this problem. The first option is to define field theories on (infinite rank) Hilbert bundles, using that such bundles are always trivializable
[
Kui65
]
; this resonates with previous definitions from
[
ST04
]
and
[
HST10
]
, and likely makes contact with Lott and Gorokhovsky’s model for differential K-theory
[
GL15
]
. The second option follows the suggestion in
[
ST11
, Remark 3.16]
, and requires a framework for superconnections defined on (not necessarily locally free) sheaves of topological vector spaces, e.g., see
[
Ulr21
]
for finite-rank results in this direction. It is not clear if either of these approaches generalize to the desired statement in dimension
2|1
.

Outline

The motivation from physics is explained in §
2
. In §
3
we define categories of superpaths in a smooth manifold. In §
4
we show that superpaths give examples of bordisms in Stolz and Teichner’s
1|1
-Euclidean bordism category from
[
ST11
]
. In §
5
we sketch a construction of the degree
n
twist and the values of the resulting degree
n
twisted field theories. The main technical results are proved in §
6
, where we restrict these values of field theories to the nearly constant superpaths to prove Proposition
1.10
. In §
7
, the relationship between cutoff theories and the families index construction leads us to the definition of the groupoids
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
. Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
then follow from standard constructions in index theory. In §
A
we review some background super algebra and super geometry, §
B
summarizes the main definitions and constructions from
[
ST11
]
used in this paper, and §
C
reviews the families index in differential
{\rm KO}
-theory from
[
BE23b
]
.

Readers without detailed knowledge of the Stolz–Teichner program may prefer to skim sections §
4
and §
5
; these sections explain how our results fit with
[
ST11
]
, but are logically independent from the main theorems.

This paper is part of a series
[
BE23a
,
BE23b
,
BE23c
]
that solves a 1-categorical version of the conjectures (
1
). The zero categorical version is the main result of
[
BE23a
]
. In the 1-categorical enhancement, the target of (
1
) requires a new model for (differential)
{\rm KO}
-theory; this is not directly related to field theories and is handled in
[
BE23b
]
, reviewed in §
C
below. The present paper connects
1|1
-Euclidean field theories with these differential KO-cocycles via an index map. In
[
BE23c
]
we generalize this index to
2|1
-dimensional field theories.

2.
Motivation from supersymmetric quantum mechanics

In this section we overview the arguments in supersymmetric quantum mechanics that lead to Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
in the case that
M={\rm pt}
. The discussion is intentionally informal with precise definitions delayed until later sections.

2.1.
Quantum mechanics on
X

To build intuition, we start by explaining ordinary quantum mechanics without supersymmetry.

Let
X
be a closed, oriented Riemannian manifold. Quantum mechanics on
X
has as its space of states
\mathcal{H}=C^{\infty}(X;{\mathbb{C}})
endowed with the hermitian inner product inherited from integration with respect to the Riemannian volume form on
X
. The Laplacian
H=\Delta
on
X
is the Hamiltonian of the theory, generating time-evolution. In the Wick-rotated theory, this gives a representation of the semigroup
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
,

(37)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\to{\rm End}(\mathcal{H}),\qquad t\mapsto e^{-tH},

where
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
is identified with a time parameter. This representation has important additional properties:
e^{-tH}
is self-adjoint, and for
t>0
the trace is finite

(38)

\displaystyle Z:={\sf Tr}(e^{-tH})\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{>0})

since
X
was assumed to be compact. The function of
t
given by (
38
) is called the
partition function
. A general quantum mechanical system is given by a semigroup representation (
37
) satisfying a self-adjoint and trace-class property.

Remark 2.1
.

Often one takes the Hilbert completion of the Fréchet space
\mathcal{H}=C^{\infty}(X;{\mathbb{C}})
, resulting in a Hilbert space of states and an unbounded Hamiltonian
H=\Delta
. For the definition of field theory from
[
ST11
]
the domain of the operator
\Delta
(as a Fréchet space) turns out to be more fundamental. By contrast, the Hilbert completion is the basic object of study for definitions of field theories in
[
ST04
]
and
[
HST10
]
.
It is unclear which point of view generalizes more readily to the
d=2
conjecture in (
1
). In this paper we will largely follow
[
ST11
]
, though we will ignore these functional analytic distinctions for the remainder of this section.

In the formalism of
[
ST04
,
HST10
,
ST11
]
, a quantum mechanical system determines a
1-dimensional Euclidean field theory
, defined as a representation of a 1-dimensional oriented Riemannian bordism category
1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}
. The objects of
1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}
are finite disjoint unions of
\bullet={\rm pt}^{+}
and
\circ={\rm pt}^{-}
, the point with its two choices of orientation. The morphisms are disjoint unions of 1-dimensional Riemannian bordisms:

(39)

{\rm pt}^{+}
{\rm pt}^{+}
{\sf I}_{t}^{+}
{\rm pt}^{-}
{\rm pt}^{-}
{\sf I}_{t}^{-}
{\rm pt}^{-}
{\rm pt}^{+}
{\sf R}_{t}^{-}
{\rm pt}^{+}
{\rm pt}^{-}
{\sf R}_{t}^{+}
{\rm pt}^{-}
{\rm pt}^{+}
{\sf L}_{t}^{+}
{\rm pt}^{+}
{\rm pt}^{-}
{\sf L}_{t}^{-}
{\sf S}_{t}

The 1-manifold
{\sf S}_{t}
is the circle of circumference
t
, viewed as a bordism from the empty set to itself.
The 1-manifolds underlying the bordisms
{\sf I}_{t}^{\pm}
,
{\sf R}_{t}^{\pm}
and
{\sf L}_{t}^{\pm}
are all intervals of length
t
; the distinctions between them come from their source and target data. The notation in (
39
) follows
[
HST10
,
ST11
]
, where
{\sf L}_{t}^{\pm}
and
{\sf R}_{t}^{\pm}
are the “left-” and “right-elbows” of length
t
, respectively, where the
+
or
-
decoration refers to the orientation, indicated pictorially in (
39
) by an arrow. We also follow the convention from
[
HST10
,
ST11
]
to read these pictures of bordisms from right to left. For example,
{\sf R}_{t}^{+}
is a bordism from the empty set to
{\rm pt}^{-}\coprod{\rm pt}^{+}
, while
{\sf L}_{t}^{+}
is a bordism from
{\rm pt}^{+}\coprod{\rm pt}^{-}
to the empty set.
Bordisms compose by gluing along matching source and target data. The disjoint union of 0- and 1-manifolds endows the category
1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}
with a symmetric monoidal structure.

One can compute relations associated with compositions of bordisms. For example, there are isometries of Riemannian 1-manifolds:

(40)

\displaystyle{\sf I}_{s}^{\pm}\circ{\sf I}_{t}^{\pm}\simeq{\sf I}_{s+t}^{\pm},\qquad{\sf L}_{0}^{\pm}\circ\sigma^{\pm}\simeq{\sf L}_{0}^{\mp},\qquad{\sf L}_{r}^{\pm}\circ({\sf I}_{t}^{\pm}\coprod{\sf I}_{s}^{\mp})\simeq{\sf L}_{r+s+t}^{\pm},

where in the middle identity
\sigma^{\pm}\colon{\rm pt}^{\mp}\coprod{\rm pt}^{\pm}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm pt}^{\pm}\coprod{\rm pt}^{\mp}
is the symmetry for the monoidal structure.
The first identity is a geometric counterpart to the semigroup
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
from (
37
): the lengths of intervals add.
Define the category
{\sf Fun}^{\otimes}(1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord},{\sf Vect})
whose objects are symmetric monoidal functors

(41)

\displaystyle E\colon 1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}\to{\sf Vect}

and morphisms are natural isomorphisms of functors. Above,
{\sf Vect}
denotes the category of super (i.e.,
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded) nuclear Fréchet spaces over
{\mathbb{C}}
endowed with the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded projective tensor product. Physically, the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-grading
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\rm ev}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{\rm odd}
keeps track of the even subspace
\mathcal{H}^{\rm ev}
as
bosonic
states and the odd subspace
\mathcal{H}^{\rm odd}
as
fermionic
states. This grading will become more important when we add supersymmetry.

For the data of a functor (
41
) to match the standard data of a quantum mechanical system (
37
),
one must demand an additional structure on the functors (
41
) called
reflection positivity
, e.g., see
[
FH21
, §3]
or
[
KS21
, page 18]
. This begins with the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-action on
1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}
generated by orientation reversal of 1-manifolds; in terms of (
39
), this is

(42)

\displaystyle{\rm pt}^{\pm}\mapsto{\rm pt}^{\mp},\qquad{\sf I}_{t}^{\pm}\mapsto{\sf I}_{t}^{\mp},\qquad{\sf R}_{t}^{\pm}\mapsto{\sf R}_{t}^{\mp},\qquad{\sf L}_{t}^{\pm}\mapsto{\sf L}_{t}^{\mp}.

Physically, this action reverses the arrow of time. There is also a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-action on vector spaces generated by complex conjugation. A
reflection structure
for the functor (
41
) is
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-equivariant data with respect to the actions on the source and target (e.g., see
[
FH21
, §B]
or Definition
B.13
below), which includes the data of an isomorphism

\mathcal{H}:=E({\rm pt}^{+})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\overline{E({\rm pt}^{-})}.

A representation (
41
) with a reflection structure is
reflection positive
if the hermitian pairing

\langle-,-\rangle\colon\overline{\mathcal{H}}\otimes\mathcal{H}\simeq E({\rm pt}^{-})\otimes E({\rm pt}^{+})\xrightarrow{E({\sf L}_{0}^{-})}{\mathbb{C}}

is positive. A
1-dimensional Euclidean field theory
is a reflection positive representation (
41
). A property demanded by reflection positivity is that
E({\sf I}_{t}^{\pm})
be a self-adjoint representation of the semigroup
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
on
\mathcal{H}
.

From the discussion above, a 1-dimensional Euclidean field theory is determined by its restriction along (
42
) as the data of a self-adjoint semigroup representation
(t\mapsto E({\sf I}_{t}^{+}))
on the vector space
\mathcal{H}=E({\rm pt}^{+})
endowed with the hermitian inner product
\langle-,-\rangle=E(L_{0}^{-})
. Furthermore, when restricted to
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
, this semigroup representation is trace class and the partition function

Z={\sf Tr}(E({\sf I}_{t}^{+}))=E({\sf S}_{t}),\qquad t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}

can be viewed as a function on the moduli of metrized circles with circumference
t>0
. Quantum mechanics on a closed, oriented Riemannian manifold
X
provides an example of a field theory with values

E({\rm pt}^{+})=C^{\infty}(X;{\mathbb{C}}),\quad E({\sf I}_{t}^{+})=e^{-t\Delta},\quad E({\sf L}_{0}^{-})(s_{1},s_{2})=\int_{X}\overline{s_{1}}s_{2}\ d{\rm vol}.

Remark 2.2
.

We comment briefly on an important technical detail ignored above. Various quantities in a 1-dimensional Euclidean field theory ought to depend smoothly on geometric parameters, e.g., the semigroup
t\mapsto E({\sf I}_{t}^{+})
and the partition function
Z=E({\sf S}_{t})
should depend smoothly on
t
. In Stolz and Teichner’s formalism
[
HST10
,
ST11
]
this is addressed by defining field theories as fibered functors, assigning smoothly varying quantities to smoothly varying families of bordisms;
see §
B
below.

2.2.
Supersymmetric quantum mechanics

Let
X
be a closed, even-dimensional Riemannian spin manifold, and
\not{S}\to X
be the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded spinor bundle with Dirac operator
\not{D}
. Supersymmetric quantum mechanics on
X
studies the space of states
\mathcal{H}=\Gamma(X;\not{S})
as a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded vector space with hermitian inner product determined by pairing and integrating spinors. The Dirac Laplacian
H=\not{D}{}^{2}
is the Hamiltonian for this theory, leading to an odd square root
Q=\not{D}
of the Hamiltonian

(43)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}[Q,Q]=\frac{1}{2}(QQ+QQ)=Q^{2}=\not{D}^{2}=H,

where
[-,-]
is the graded commutator. The operator
Q
defines a
supersymmetry
, e.g., see
[
Wit82
, §1]
. A first consequence of supersymmetry is that the partition function

(44)

\displaystyle Z={\sf sTr}(e^{-tH})={\sf sTr}(e^{-tQ^{2}})=\dim(\ker(Q))_{+}-\dim(\ker(Q))_{-}={\rm Ind}(Q)

is independent of
t
and equal to the super dimension of the kernel of
Q
, i.e., the index of the Dirac operator
Q=\not{D}
. This follows from the McKean–Singer formula
[
MS67
]
; see also
[
BGV92
, Theorem 3.50]
. It is crucial in this argument that the partition function (
44
) uses the
supertrace
, defined as

{\sf sTr}(e^{-tH}):={\sf Tr}((-1)^{\sf F}\circ e^{-tH})

where
{\sf Tr}
is the ordinary trace, and
(-1)^{\sf F}
acts by
+1
on the even subspace of
\mathcal{H}
and
-1
on the odd subspace.

The supersymmetry
Q
allows one to extend the usual time-evolution (
37
) to a representation of a
super semigroup
,

(45)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}\to{\rm End}(\mathcal{H})\qquad(t,\theta)\mapsto e^{-tH+\theta Q},

where
(t,\theta)
are coordinates on the supermanifold with boundary
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}
, defined by restricting the structure sheaf of
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
along the inclusion
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
(see Example
A.12
). The semigroup multiplication on
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}
is the restriction of multiplication in the super Lie group defined as follows.

Definition 2.3
.

Define the super Lie group
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
with multiplication

(46)

\displaystyle(t,\theta)\cdot(s,\eta)=(t+s+\theta\eta,\theta+\eta)\qquad(t,\theta),(s,\eta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}(S),

where the above formula is understood in terms of the functor of points of
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
.

Remark 2.4
.

A closely related (and perhaps better known) example of supersymmetric quantum mechanics takes as its space of states
\mathcal{H}=\Omega^{\bullet}(X)
, differential forms on an oriented Riemannian manifold
[
Wit82
, §2]
. The Hamiltonian is then defined to be the Hodge Laplacian
H=dd^{*}+d^{*}d
, which has a pair of commuting odd square roots,

(47)

\displaystyle Q_{1}=d+d^{*},\quad Q_{2}=i(d-d^{*}),\quad[Q_{1},Q_{2}]=0,\quad Q_{1}^{2}=Q_{2}^{2}=H.

This is the data of
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetry, rather than the
\mathcal{N}=1
example above. With our intended connections to index theory,
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetry is more fundamental. Of course, forgetting one of the
Q_{i}
in (
47
) extracts an
\mathcal{N}=1
theory.

A general supersymmetric quantum mechanical system is given by a supersemigroup representation (
45
) satisfying a self-adjoint and trace class condition. In the formalism of
[
ST04
,
HST10
,
ST11
]
, such data determine a
1|1
-dimensional Euclidean field theory
, as we review below.

One can repackage supersymmetric quantum mechanics on
X
as a representation of a
1|1
-Euclidean bordism category
1|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}
, arising as a generalization of
1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}
. The objects of
1|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}
are finite disjoint unions of
\bullet={\rm spt}^{+}
and
\circ={\rm spt}^{-}
, the
super point
with its two choices of orientation. The morphisms can be visualized as

(48)

{\rm spt}^{+}
{\rm spt}^{+}
{\sf I}_{t,\theta}^{+}
{\rm spt}^{-}
{\rm spt}^{-}
{\sf I}_{t,\theta}^{-}
{\rm spt}^{-}
{\rm spt}^{+}
{\sf R}_{t,\theta}^{-}
{\rm spt}^{+}
{\rm spt}^{-}
{\sf R}_{t,\theta}^{+}
{\rm spt}^{-}
{\rm spt}^{+}
{\sf L}_{t,\theta}^{+}
{\rm spt}^{+}
{\rm spt}^{-}
{\sf L}_{t,\theta}^{-}
{\sf S}_{t,\theta}^{-}
{\sf S}_{t,\theta}^{+}

much the same as (
39
), but where the parameter
t
measuring the Riemannian length is replaced by a pair
(t,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
measuring a “super” length. This super length has the usual even parameter
t
, but also an odd parameter
\theta
; these parameters determine (super) families of bordisms as we explain in §
3.3
. It turns out to be convenient
1
1

1

The isomorphism
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}\simeq({\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\leq 0})^{\sf{op}}
equivalently allows one to view the bordisms
{\sf I}^{-}_{t,\theta},{\sf R}^{-}_{t,\theta},{\sf L}^{-}_{t,\theta}
and
{\sf S}^{-}_{t,\theta}
with
(t,\theta)
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}
, but then compositions involving these bordisms use the opposite super semigroup structure. This is a non-issue for ordinary Euclidean bordisms, since
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
and
{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}
are isomorphic semigroups.
for
{\sf I}^{+}_{t,\theta},{\sf R}^{+}_{t,\theta},{\sf L}^{+}_{t,\theta}
and
{\sf S}^{+}_{t,\theta}
to have super length parameters
(t,\theta)
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}
, whereas
{\sf I}^{-}_{t,\theta},{\sf R}^{-}_{t,\theta},{\sf L}^{-}_{t,\theta}
and
{\sf S}^{-}_{t,\theta}
have
(t,\theta)
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\leq 0}
. The supermanifolds underlying the bordisms
{\sf I}_{t,\theta}^{\pm}
,
{\sf R}_{t,\theta}^{\pm}
and
{\sf L}_{t,\theta}^{\pm}
are again all the same, being super intervals. We similarly find relations associated with compositions of bordisms, e.g.,

(49)

\displaystyle{\sf I}_{s,\eta}^{\pm}\circ{\sf I}_{t,\theta}^{\pm}\simeq{\sf I}_{s+t+\eta\theta,\eta+\theta}^{\pm},\quad{\sf L}_{0}^{\pm}\circ\sigma^{\pm}\simeq{\sf L}_{0}^{\mp},\quad{\sf L}_{0}^{\pm}\circ({\sf I}_{t,\theta}^{\pm}\coprod{\sf I}_{s,\eta}^{\mp})\simeq{\sf L}_{(s,\eta)^{-1}(t,\theta)}^{\pm}

where in the middle identity,
\sigma^{\pm}\colon{\rm spt}^{\mp}\coprod{\rm spt}^{\pm}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm spt}^{\pm}\coprod{\rm spt}^{\mp}
is again the symmetry for the monoidal structure.
The first identity is the geometric counterpart to multiplication in the super semigroups
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{1|1}
from (
45
). Note that the gluing of super intervals encodes a non-commutative super group; this is expected as (
43
) is not an abelian super Lie algebra.

With this in place, we may consider the category
{\sf Fun}^{\otimes}(1|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord},{\sf Vect})
whose objects are symmetric monoidal functors

(50)

\displaystyle E\colon 1|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}\to{\sf Vect}.

For the data of a functor (
50
) to properly match with the physicists notion of a supersymmetric quantum mechanical theory, we again must impose a version of reflection positivity; this is defined analogously to the case without supersymmetry. We define a
1|1
-dimensional Euclidean field theory
to be a reflection-positive functor (
50
).
One consequence is that a
1|1
-dimensional Euclidean field theory determines a self-adjoint representation of the super semigroup
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{1|1}
on a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded topological vector space
\mathcal{H}
with hermitian inner product
\langle-,-\rangle
. This data satisfies a trace property on
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{1|1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}
, where the trace is the value of the functor (
50
) on a super circle (which turns out to be a constant function of
(t,\theta)
). Supersymmetric quantum mechanics determines a reflection positive functor (
50
) with values

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}E({\rm spt}^{+})=\Gamma(X;\not{S}),\quad E({\sf I}_{t,\theta}^{+})=e^{-t\not{D}+\theta\not{D}},\quad E({\sf L}_{0}^{-})(s_{1},s_{2})=\int_{X}(s_{1},s_{2})d{\rm vol}.\end{array}

Remark 2.5
.

Restricting (
45
) along the homomorphism
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}
of semigroups forgets the supersymmetry, extracting an ordinary quantum mechanical theory. Similarly, any
1|1
-dimensional Euclidean field theory determines a 1-dimensional Euclidean field theory; see
[
ST11
, Equation 4.14]
.

Remark 2.6
.

Supersymmetric quantum mechanics can be understood entirely from the vantage of super Lie algebra representations (
43
) or super semigroup representations (
45
). Our primary motivation for pursuing a description in terms of the
1|1
-Euclidean bordism category is that there is an evident
2|1
-Euclidean generalization, illuminating a candidate definition for
2|1
-Euclidean field theory in the conjecture (
1
).

2.3.
The renormalization group flow, low-energy cutoffs, and K-theory

Given
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
, there is a functor
1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}\to 1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}
that dilates the metric on Euclidean bordisms by the factor
\mu
. In terms of the elementary bordisms (
39
), this has the effect

\displaystyle{\rm pt}^{\pm}\mapsto{\rm pt}^{\pm},\qquad{\sf I}_{t}^{\pm}\mapsto{\sf I}_{\mu t}^{\pm},\qquad{\sf R}_{t}^{\pm}\mapsto{\sf R}_{\mu t}^{\pm},\qquad{\sf L}_{t}^{\pm}\mapsto{\sf L}_{\mu t}^{\pm},\quad{\sf S}_{t}\mapsto{\sf S}_{\mu t}.

Precomposing a functor (
41
) with this action defines the
renormalization group flow
acting on 1-dimensional Euclidean field theories. A field theory is invariant under the renormalization group (RG) flow if and only if
E({\sf I}_{t}^{+})={{\rm id}}_{\mathcal{H}}
for all
t
. The trace class condition on
E({\sf I}_{t}^{+})
implies that RG-invariant field theories have a finite-dimensional space of states
\mathcal{H}
, and hence determine a 1-dimensional topological field theory.

Similarly, there are functors
1|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}\to 1|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}
that dilate the super lengths as
(t,\theta)\mapsto(\mu^{2}t,\mu\theta)
. On the elementary bordisms (
48
) this gives

\displaystyle{\rm pt}^{\pm}\mapsto{\rm pt}^{\pm},\qquad{\sf I}_{t,\theta}^{\pm}\mapsto{\sf I}_{\mu^{2}t,\mu\theta}^{\pm},\qquad{\sf R}_{t,\theta}^{\pm}\mapsto{\sf R}_{\mu^{2}t,\mu\theta}^{\pm},\qquad{\sf L}_{t,\theta}^{\pm}\mapsto{\sf L}_{\mu^{2}t,\mu\theta}^{\pm},\quad{\sf S}_{t,\theta}^{\pm}\mapsto{\sf S}_{\mu^{2}t,\mu\theta}^{\pm}.

The nontrivial action on the odd part of the super length
\theta
is necessary to make the dilation action compatible with composition (and hence a functor). Precomposing (
50
) with the above functor defines the renormalization group flow on
1|1
-dimensional super Euclidean field theories. In the presence of supersymmetry, the partition function is invariant under the renormalization group flow: it is a constant function of the geometric parameters
(t,\theta)
.

Given a quantum mechanical system of the form (
37
) or (
45
), let
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}\subset\mathcal{H}
denote the
\lambda
-eigenspace of the self-adjoint Hamiltonian
H
(so
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
). Then
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
is the space of
states with energy
\lambda
. Define the vector space of states with energy
<\lambda
,

(53)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}:=\bigoplus_{\nu<\lambda}\mathcal{H}_{\nu}

with hermitian inner product given by the restriction of the inner product on
\mathcal{H}
to the subspaces
\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}\subset\mathcal{H}
. The trace class condition on
e^{-tH}
implies that eigenspaces of
H
are finite-dimensional, the spectrum of
H
is discrete, and
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}=\{0\}
for
\lambda\ll 0
. Hence,
\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}
is finite-dimensional. Define
\lambda
-cutoff theory
as the quantum mechanical system given by the finite-dimensional vector space
\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}
equipped with the identity time-evolution operator. The
\lambda
-cutoff theory is therefore invariant under the renormalization group flow, i.e., a 1-dimensional topological field theory.

Remark 2.7
.

One could equally equip
\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}
with the restricted Hamiltonian
H|_{\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}}
when defining the cutoff theory.
For our purposes, it turns out to be more convenient for the cutoff theory to be RG-invariant.

Since
\mathcal{H}
is
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded and
H
is an even map,
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
is
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded for each
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Given a fixed choice of
\lambda
and 1- or
1|1
-dimensional Euclidean field theory
E
, consider the assignment

(54)

\displaystyle E\mapsto[\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}]=[(\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda})^{\rm ev}]-[(\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda})^{\rm odd}]\in{\rm K}^{0}({\rm pt}).

In the absence of supersymmetry, the map (
54
) generally depends on
\lambda
. However, when
H
has an odd square root
Q
, then the assignment is independent of
\lambda
:
Q
gives isomorphisms

Q\colon\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}^{{\rm ev}/{\rm odd}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}^{{\rm odd}/{\rm ev}},\qquad\lambda\neq 0

and so

[\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}]=[\mathcal{H}_{0}]\in{\rm K}^{0}({\rm pt})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}

for all
\lambda
. This integer-valued invariant in supersymmetric quantum mechanics is the
Witten index
, originally studied in
[
Wit82
]
. The Witten index is a deformation invariant of a
1|1
-dimensional Euclidean field theory.

Example 2.8
.

For the theory with
Q=\not{D}
the Dirac operator on an even dimensional spin manifold, the Witten index agrees with the analytic index
{\rm Ind}(\not{D})=[\mathcal{H}_{0}]\in{\rm K}^{0}({\rm pt})
of the Dirac operator. In the example discussed in Remark
2.4
, the Witten index is the Euler characteristic of
X
.

By contrast, 1-dimensional Euclidean field theories without supersymmetry form a contractible space
[
HST10
, Theorem 6.35]
and so have no interesting deformation invariants.

Remark 2.9
.

We note that the McKean–Singer formula (
44
) equates the Witten index and the partition function as elements of
{\mathbb{Z}}
. In families, this identification is modified: the partition function is a differential form which agrees with the Chern character of the Witten index.

2.4.
Symmetries and real structures

Given a quantum mechanical system in the form (
37
) or (
45
), a super algebra
A
acting on
\mathcal{H}
is an algebra of
symmetries
if it commutes with the action of
H
or
Q
. Reflection positivity for a theory with
A
-symmetry again endows
\mathcal{H}
with a hermitian inner product, and additionally requires the data of a
*
-structure on
A
and the property that the
A
-action on
\mathcal{H}
be a
*
-action.

It is often convenient to organize field theories according to their symmetries: for a given super algebra
A
one can consider the collection of theories with
A
-symmetry. Stolz and Teichner’s degree
n
field theories are supersymmetric quantum mechanical systems (
45
) with symmetry specified by the the
n
th (complex) Clifford algebra

\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\langle f_{1},\dots f_{n}\mid[f_{j},f_{k}]=-2\delta_{jk}\rangle&n\geq 0\\
\langle e_{1},\dots e_{n}\mid[e_{j},e_{k}]=2\delta_{jk}\rangle&n<0.\end{array}\right.

where
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
or
\{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\}
denotes the standard basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. These Clifford algebras carry a standard
*
-structure; see §
A.2
.

One can also ask for a
real structure
on a quantum mechanical system, meaning a grading-preserving
{\mathbb{C}}
-antilinear involution of
\mathcal{H}
commuting with the Hamiltonian. When considering theories with
A
-symmetry, one should demand a compatible real structure on
A
. In the case of the Clifford algebras, this is equivalent to declaring the generators in (
2.4
) to be real so that the real structure is determined by complex conjugation.
Using the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro description of
{\rm KO}^{n}({\rm pt})
in terms of Clifford modules
[
ABS64
]
, such real, degree
n
theories determine classes in
{\rm KO}^{n}({\rm pt})
using the construction (
54
).

Examples of real, degree
n
theories arise naturally in spin geometry.
Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional spin manifold with principal spin bundle
P
, i.e.,
P\to X
is a
{\rm Spin}(n)
-principal bundle that double covers of the oriented frame bundle of
TX
. Define the spinor bundle as

(58)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{S}}:=P\times_{{\rm Spin}(n)}{\rm Cl}_{n}=P\times_{{\rm Spin}(n)}({\rm Cl}_{n}^{+}\oplus{\rm Cl}_{n}^{-})

with
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-grading inherited from the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-grading on the real Clifford algebra
{\rm Cl}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})={\rm Cl}_{n}
. This version of the spinor bundle carries a right
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-action, which we will identify with a left
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-action (see Example
A.8
). The odd, self-adjoint,
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-linear Dirac operator
\not{D}
acts on global sections
\Gamma(X;{\mathbb{S}})
. The same constructions as before give a quantum mechanical system with additional structures: the space of states
\mathcal{H}=\Gamma(X;\not{S})
is a real, self-adjoint
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-module and the operator
Q=\not{D}
commutes with the Clifford action.
The energy cutoff (
53
) gives a finite-dimensional Clifford module. Via the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro construction, the assignment (
54
) is the
Clifford index
of the Dirac operator, taking values in
{\rm KO}^{-n}({\rm pt})
. This fundamental geometric example motivates Stolz and Teichner’s definitions of degree
n
field theories in dimension
1|1
, see
[
ST04
, Example 2.3.3]
.

Remark 2.10
.

For an inner product space
W
, the Clifford algebra
{\rm Cl}(W)
can be understood as the quantization of a free (fermionic) particle moving in the odd vector space
\Pi W
, e.g., see
[
Fre99
, page 19]
. Free fermions in higher dimensions illuminate a path towards a general framework for degree
n
field theories. In particular, this suggests a
2|1
-dimensional generalization for the second conjecture in (
1
).

3.
Super Euclidean paths in a manifold

3.1.
Warm-up: The category of (non-super) Euclidean paths in a manifold

Let
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
be the affine real line, regarded as an oriented Riemannian manifold. The isometry group of
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
is the Lie group
{\mathbb{R}}
, acting on
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
by translation. We will describe Euclidean paths modeled on
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
with an eye towards the generalization to
1|1
-Euclidean paths.

The Riemannian manifold
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
, isometry group
{\mathbb{R}}
, and a choice of connected codimension 1 submanifold
{\mathbb{A}}^{0}\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
determines the 1-dimensional Euclidean geometry
({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{A}}^{1},{\mathbb{A}}^{0})
in the sense of Stolz and Teichner
[
ST11
, §2.5]
; see §
B.1
for a review. Below, for
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
let
T_{s}\colon{\mathbb{A}}^{1}\to{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
denote translation by
s
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold. A
positively oriented Euclidean path
in
M
is a triple
(\phi,t_{\rm in},t_{\rm out})
for a smooth map
\phi\colon{\mathbb{A}}^{1}\to M
and points
t_{\rm in},t_{\rm out}\in{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
with
t_{\rm out}-t_{\rm in}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
. Similarly, a
negatively oriented Euclidean path
in
M
is a triple
(\phi,t_{\rm in},t_{\rm out})
with
t_{\rm out}-t_{\rm in}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}
. A
Euclidean path
is a positively or negatively oriented Euclidean path. The point
\phi(t_{\rm in})\in M
is the
incoming
boundary and
\phi(t_{\rm out})\in M
is the
outgoing
boundary of a Euclidean path.
An
isometry

(\phi,t_{\rm in},t_{\rm out})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(\phi^{\prime},t_{\rm in}^{\prime},t_{\rm out}^{\prime})
between a pair of Euclidean paths is the data of
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\phi^{\prime}=\phi\circ T_{s}^{-1}
,
T_{s}(t_{\rm in})=t_{\rm in}^{\prime}
, and
T_{s}(t_{\rm out})=t_{\rm out}^{\prime}
.

Euclidean paths and isometries form a groupoid that is equivalent to a discrete groupoid (i.e., a groupoid with only identity morphisms) whose objects are

(59)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M)\coprod{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M),

where we regard
{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M)
as a presheaf on the site of manifolds,

{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M)\colon{\sf Mfld}^{\sf{op}}\to{\sf Set},\qquad S\mapsto C^{\infty}(S\times{\mathbb{R}},M).

Indeed, for a given Euclidean path
(\phi,t_{\rm in},t_{\rm out})
, the basepoint
t_{\rm in}\in{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
gives an identification
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{1}
, so that
\phi\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to M
and
t_{\rm out}
is determined by a point in
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\coprod{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}
.

The 1-dimensional Euclidean geometry has some additional structures that lead to additional structures on Euclidean paths. First, the basepoint
{\mathbb{A}}^{0}\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
provides an isomorphism
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{1}
(that may be different than the identification using
t_{\rm in}
). This gives a preferred orientation-reversing map of
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
,

(60)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}^{1}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{1}\xrightarrow{{\sf or}}{\mathbb{R}}^{1}\simeq{\mathbb{A}}^{1},\qquad{\sf or}(t)=-t,\ t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Next, for any
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
there is a diffeomorphism

(61)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}^{1}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{1}\xrightarrow{{\sf rg}_{\mu}}{\mathbb{R}}^{1}\simeq{\mathbb{A}}^{1},\qquad{\sf rg}_{\mu}(t)=\mu\cdot t

that dilates the metric on
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
. The notation
{\sf rg}_{\mu}
is in reference to the connection with the renormalization group. We emphasize that (
60
) and (
61
) are not isometries in the 1-dimensional Euclidean geometry
({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{A}}^{1},{\mathbb{A}}^{0})
.

The structures (
60
) and (
61
) provide the following additional structures on Euclidean paths. The
orientation reversal
of a Euclidean path
(\phi,t_{\rm in},t_{\rm out})
applies the orientation-reversing isometry (
60
), giving an assignment

(\phi,t_{\rm in},t_{\rm out})\mapsto(\phi\circ{\sf or}^{-1},{\sf or}(t_{\rm in}),{\sf or}(t_{\rm out})).

For
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
the
\mu
-dilation
of a Euclidean path
(\phi,t_{\rm in},t_{\rm out})
applies the diffeomorphism (
61
) to a Euclidean path so that

(\phi,t_{\rm in},t_{\rm out})\mapsto(\phi\circ{\sf rg}_{\mu}^{-1},{\sf rg}_{\mu}(t_{\rm in}),{\sf rg}_{\mu}(t_{\rm out})).

In terms of the description (
59
) of Euclidean paths, orientation reversal and
\mu
-dilation are

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M)\coprod{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M)\coprod{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M)

(62)

\displaystyle(t,\phi)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(-t,\phi\circ{\sf or}^{-1})

\displaystyle(t,\phi)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(\mu\cdot t,\phi\circ{\sf rg}_{\mu}^{-1}).

In particular, orientation reversal exchanges positively and negatively oriented Euclidean paths. The incoming and outgoing boundary
\phi(t_{\rm in}),\phi(t_{\rm out})\in M
are preserved by orientation reversal and
\mu
-dilation.

Next, we consider the extent to which Euclidean paths can be viewed as morphisms in a category. There are maps

{\rm in},{\rm out}\colon{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M)\coprod{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M)\rightrightarrows M\coprod M

sending a Euclidean path to its incoming or outgoing boundary. We would like the above to determine the source and target maps in a category in which composition comes from concatenation of paths. However, this runs into a problem: concatenation is only piecewise smooth. There are different ways to resolve this problem. One option is to consider collared paths in
M
, which is the route followed by Stolz and Teichner
[
ST11
]
; see §
B.3
. Another option is to encode cutting laws rather than gluing laws as in
[
DG20
,
LS21
,
BEP22
]
. The resulting bordism categories are not equivalent, and it isn’t clear which one is “correct” when generalizing the quantum mechanical systems discussed in §
2.1
. However, there is a subspace of (
59
) where the difficulties of concatenation disappear: the
constant
Euclidean paths in
M
.

Definition 3.2
.

Define the (Lie)
category of constant paths in
M
, denoted
{\sf Path}_{0}(M)
, as having objects and morphisms the smooth manifolds

{\rm Ob}({\sf Path}_{0}(M))=M\coprod M,\qquad{\rm Mor}({\sf Path}_{0}(M))={\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\times M\coprod{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}\times M.

The source and target maps in
{\sf Path}_{0}(M)
are given by the projections.
The unit map is determined by inclusion at
0\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and composition is determined by addition in
{\mathbb{R}}
.

Under the inclusion of the constant maps

{\rm Mor}({\sf Path}_{0}(M))={\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\times M\coprod{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}\times M\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M)\coprod{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M)

concatenation of paths corresponds to composition in the category
{\sf Path}_{0}(M)
. The assignments (
62
) furthermore extend to functors

(63)

\displaystyle{\sf Or}\colon{\sf Path}_{0}(M)\to{\sf Path}_{0}(M),\quad{\sf RG}_{\mu}\colon{\sf Path}_{0}(M)\to{\sf Path}_{0}(M).

Next we explain the relationship between Euclidean paths and Euclidean loops. There is a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-action on (
59
) given by
(t,\phi)\mapsto(t,n_{t}\cdot\phi)
where
n_{t}\cdot\phi\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to M
is the map

(n_{t}\cdot\phi)(s)=\phi(s+nt),\qquad n\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

For paths of strictly positively length, the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-fixed subspace (as a presheaf) is the
Euclidean loop space
of
M

\mathcal{L}(M):=({\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M)\coprod{\mathbb{R}}_{<0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M))^{\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M)\coprod{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}},M)

whose points
(t,\phi)
are loops in
M
of circumference
t>0
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{L}(M)
has an
S^{1}
-action by rotation of loops. Any constant path of positive length determines a loop, giving a span

(64)

\displaystyle{\rm Mor}({\sf Path}_{0}(M))\hookleftarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times M\coprod{\mathbb{R}}_{<0}\times M\hookrightarrow\mathcal{L}(M),

and the
S^{1}
-action on
\mathcal{L}(M)
restricts to the trivial action on
{\rm Mor}({\sf Path}_{0}(M))
.

In summary, although constant Euclidean paths lack the geometric richness of the full path space, they provide two technical simplifications: (1) constant paths form a finite-dimensional smooth manifold, and (2) concatenation of constant paths always results in a (smooth) constant path. When
M={\rm pt}
, the category
{\sf Path}_{0}(M)
recovers the semigroup of intervals (
37
), and hence the categories
{\sf Path}_{0}(M)
(and their representations) offer one possible families-generalization of the quantum mechanical systems studied previously. With this in mind, we purse a generalization of the above; the goal of the remainder of the section is to generalize Definition
3.2
to a category of (nearly) constant super Euclidean paths in
M
.

3.2.
1|1
-dimensional Euclidean geometry

Recall from (
46
) the super Lie group
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
, and let
{\mathbb{A}}^{1|1}
denote the (affine) supermanifold underlying the super Lie group
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
. This notation emphasizes that
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
has a distinguished basepoint (the identity
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
) whereas
{\mathbb{A}}^{1|1}
does not. Similarly, let
{\mathbb{A}}^{0|1}
denote the supermanifold underlying the super Lie group
{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}
. Let
{\mathbb{A}}^{0|1}\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{1|1}
be the embedding modeled on the standard
2
2

2

We caution that
{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
is not a homomorphism for the group structure (
46
) on
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
.
inclusion
{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
. We again refer to §
B.1
for a review of rigid super geometries.

Definition 3.3

(
[
HST10
, Example 6.16]
and
[
ST11
, §4.2]
)
.

Form the semidirect product
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
where
{\mathbb{Z}}/2=\{\pm 1\}
acts on
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
by
(t,\theta)\mapsto(t,\pm\theta)
. The

1|1
-Euclidean geometry
is specified by the triple

(G,{\mathbb{M}},{\mathbb{M}}^{c})=({\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2,{\mathbb{A}}^{1|1},{\mathbb{A}}^{0|1})

for the evident action of
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
on
{\mathbb{A}}^{1|1}
and the inclusion
{\mathbb{A}}^{0|1}\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{1|1}
described above.

In the notation of Definition
B.2
, the stack
({\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2,{\mathbb{A}}^{1|1})
-
{\sf SMfld}
of
1|1
-Euclidean manifolds has as objects submersions
Y\to S
whose fibers are
1|1
-dimensional manifolds endowed with a super Euclidean structure. The stack of
1|1
-Euclidean pairs has as objects fiber bundles
Y\to S
of
1|1
-manifolds endowed with super Euclidean structure together with a family of embedded
0|1
-dimensional supermanifolds
Y^{c}\subset Y
over
S
.

Example 3.4
.

Our main example of an
S
-family of
1|1
-Euclidean pairs depends on
(t,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}(S)
and is given by

(65)

\displaystyle Y^{c}=S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\xhookrightarrow{i_{t,\theta}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}=Y

where

(66)

\displaystyle i_{t,\theta}\colon S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\subset S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\xrightarrow{T_{t,\theta}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}

using the standard inclusion and the super translation map
T_{t,\theta}
defined as the composition

(67)

\displaystyle S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\xrightarrow{{{\rm id}}_{S}\times(t,\theta)\times{{\rm id}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\xrightarrow{{{\rm id}}_{S}\times{\rm act}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1},

where the second arrow is the action of
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
on itself. For any
(t,\theta)
and
(s,\eta)
the associated
1|1
-Euclidean pairs are isomorphic via the isometry

S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\xrightarrow{T_{(s,\eta)\cdot(t,\theta)^{-1}}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}.

In particular, all pairs (
65
) are isomorphic to the standard one
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\subset S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
.

The stack of
1|1
-dimensional super Euclidean manifolds has some extra structures generalizing orientation reversal (
60
) and dilation (
61
) of ordinary Euclidean 1-manifolds.

Definition 3.5
.

Orientation reversal
is the automorphism of the
1|1
-Euclidean geometry determined by the homomorphism

(68)

\displaystyle{\sf or}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1},

\displaystyle\qquad(t,\theta)\mapsto(-t,-i\theta),\qquad(t,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}(S).

We observe that when restricted to
{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
(
68
) is the standard orientation reversing map (
60
) on
{\mathbb{R}}
.

Remark 3.6
.

The map (
68
) is called the
pin generator
in
[
HST10
, Example 6.16]
.

Definition 3.7
.

For
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
, the
renormalization group action
is the automorphism of the
1|1
-Euclidean geometry determined by the homomorphism

(69)

\displaystyle{\sf rg}_{\mu}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1},

\displaystyle\qquad(t,\theta)\mapsto(\mu^{2}t,\mu\theta),\qquad(t,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}(S).

Definition 3.8

(
[
ST11
, Example 2.39 and §4.1]
)
.

The
spin flip
is the automorphism of the
1|1
-Euclidean geometry determined by the action of
-1\in\{\pm 1\}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}/2<{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2

(70)

\displaystyle{\sf fl}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1},\qquad(t,\theta)\mapsto(t,-\theta),\qquad(t,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}(S).

Since
{\mathbb{Z}}/2<{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
is in the center, the spin flip commutes with the action of the isometry group.

The
1|1
-Euclidean geometry also has a real structure in the sense of Definition
B.5
.

Lemma 3.9

(
[
HST10
, Example 6.20]
)
.

The super Lie group
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
has a real structure

(71)

\displaystyle{\sf r}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\xrightarrow{\sim}\overline{{\mathbb{R}}}^{1|1},\qquad\overline{{\sf r}}\circ{\sf r}={{\rm id}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}}.

Proof.

Following Example
A.19
, first choose an identification
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\simeq\Pi(\underline{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\mathbb{C}})
where
\underline{{\mathbb{R}}}
is the trivial real line bundle on
{\mathbb{R}}
and
\underline{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is its complexification. This endows the supermanifold underlying
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
with a real structure. To promote this to a real structure on the super Lie group, let
\theta\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{1|1})^{\rm odd}\simeq\Gamma({\mathbb{R}},\Lambda^{1}(\underline{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\mathbb{C}}))
denote the function associated with a (real) trivialization of
\underline{{\mathbb{R}}}
. With this choice, the group multiplication (
46
) is real.
∎

Definition 3.10
.

Define a real structure on the
1|1
-dimensional super Euclidean model geometry from the real structure on the super Lie group
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2
from Lemma
3.9
, using that
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
preserves this real structure on
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
.

Example 3.11
.

By Lemma
B.4
, the maps (
68
), (
69
) and (
70
) determine endofunctors of the stack of
1|1
-Euclidean manifolds,

{\sf Or},{\sf RG}_{\mu},{\sf Fl}\colon({\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2,{\mathbb{A}}^{1|1})\hbox{-}{\sf SMfld}\to({\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2,{\mathbb{A}}^{1|1})\hbox{-}{\sf SMfld},

that send an
S
-family of
1|1
-Euclidean manifolds to another
S
-family. Applied to the super Euclidean pair (
65
), we find

\displaystyle{\sf Or}(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\xhookrightarrow{i_{t,\theta}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\xhookrightarrow{i_{-t,-i\theta}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1})

\displaystyle{\sf RG}_{\mu}(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\xhookrightarrow{i_{t,\theta}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\xhookrightarrow{i_{\mu^{2}t,\mu\theta}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1})

\displaystyle{\sf Fl}(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\xhookrightarrow{i_{t,\theta}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\xhookrightarrow{i_{t,-\theta}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1})

Similarly, the map (
71
) determines a map of stacks

{\sf R}\colon({\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2,{\mathbb{A}}^{1|1})\hbox{-}{\sf SMfld}\to({\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}/2,{\mathbb{A}}^{1|1})\hbox{-}{\sf SMfld},\quad(Y^{c},Y)\mapsto(\overline{Y}{}^{c},\overline{Y})

covering complex conjugation of supermanifolds, that sends an
S
-family of
1|1
-Euclidean manifolds to an
\overline{S}
-family. There are natural isomorphisms of functors

(72)

\displaystyle{\sf Or}^{2}\simeq{\sf Fl},\quad{\sf RG}_{\mu}\circ{\sf RG}_{\mu^{\prime}}\simeq{\sf RG}_{\mu\mu^{\prime}},\quad{\sf R}\circ{\sf Or}\simeq\overline{{\sf Fl}}\circ\overline{{\sf Or}}\circ{\sf R},\quad\overline{{\sf R}}\circ{\sf R}\simeq{{\rm id}}

coming from the equalities of maps

{\sf or}^{2}={\sf fl},\quad{\sf rg}_{\mu}\circ{\sf rg}_{\mu^{\prime}}={\sf rg}_{\mu\mu^{\prime}},\quad\overline{{\sf or}}\circ{\sf r}=\overline{{\sf fl}}\circ{\sf r}\circ{\sf or},\quad\overline{{\sf r}}\circ{\sf r}={{\rm id}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}}.

3.3.
Super Euclidean paths in a smooth manifold

Given a triple
(\phi,(t,\theta),(s,\eta))
where
\phi\colon S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\to M
is a map and
(t,\theta),(s,\eta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}(S)
, consider

(73)

S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}
M
i_{t,\theta}
i_{s,\eta}
\phi

for the maps defined in (
66
).

Definition 3.12
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold and
S
a supermanifold. An
S
-family of
positively oriented super Euclidean paths
in
M
is a triple
(\phi,(t_{\rm out},\theta_{\rm out}),(t_{\rm in},\theta_{\rm in}))
determining a diagram (
73
) with the property

\displaystyle(t_{\rm out},\theta_{\rm out})\cdot(t_{\rm in},\theta_{\rm in})^{-1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}(S)\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}(S).

Similarly, an
S
-family of
negatively oriented super Euclidean paths
in
M
is a triple where

\displaystyle(t_{\rm out},\theta_{\rm out})\cdot(t_{\rm in},\theta_{\rm in})^{-1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\leq 0}(S)\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}(S).

An
S
-family of
super Euclidean paths
in
M
is a triple
(\phi,(t_{\rm out},\theta_{\rm out}),(t_{\rm in},\theta_{\rm in}))
that over each component of
S
is a family of positively or negatively oriented superpaths. The
incoming
and
outgoing
boundaries of
(\phi,(t,\theta),(s,\eta))
are the compositions

\phi_{\rm out}\colon S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\xhookrightarrow{i_{(t_{\rm out},\theta_{\rm out})}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\xrightarrow{\phi}M,\quad\phi_{\rm in}\colon S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\xhookrightarrow{i_{(t_{\rm in},\theta_{\rm in})}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\xrightarrow{\phi}M.

An
isometry

(\phi,(t_{\rm out},\theta_{\rm out}),(t_{\rm in},\theta_{\rm in}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(\phi^{\prime},(t_{\rm out}^{\prime},\theta_{\rm out}^{\prime}),(t_{\rm in}^{\prime},\theta_{\rm in}^{\prime}))
between a pair of
S
-families of superpaths is the data of
(s,\eta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}(S)
such that

\phi^{\prime}=\phi\circ T_{s,\eta},\quad T_{s,\eta}\circ\phi_{\rm in}=\phi_{\rm in}^{\prime},\quad T_{s,\eta}\circ\phi_{\rm out}=\phi_{\rm out}^{\prime}

where
T_{s,\eta}
is the super translation by
(s,\eta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}(S)
defined in (
67
).

Super Euclidean paths in
M
and their isometries form a stack: for each
S
we have a groupoid of
S
-families of super Euclidean paths over
S
, and for a map
f\colon S^{\prime}\to S
of supermanifolds we obtain a functor between groupoids by pulling back the triples
(\phi,(t_{\rm out},\theta_{\rm out}),(t_{\rm in},\theta_{\rm in}))
along
f
. Analogously to (
59
), we have the following.

Lemma 3.13
.

The groupoid of super Euclidean paths over
S
is equivalent to the discrete groupoid given by the value of the sheaf

(74)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{1|1},M)\coprod{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\leq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{1|1},M)

on the supermanifold
S
, and hence the stack of super Euclidean paths in
M
is equivalent to the sheaf (
74
).

Proof.

Given an
S
-family of positively (respectively, negatively) oriented super Euclidean paths
(\phi,(t_{\rm out},\theta_{\rm out}),(t_{\rm in},\theta_{\rm in}))
, there is a unique isomorphism to a superpath with
(t_{\rm in},\theta_{\rm in})=(0,0)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}(S)
. With this identification fixed, the remaining data is

(75)

\displaystyle\phi\colon S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\to M,\qquad(t_{\rm out},\theta_{\rm out})=(t,\theta)\in({\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}\coprod{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\leq 0})(S).

The result follows.
∎

3.4.
The category of nearly constant superpaths

Concatenation of
S
-families of superpaths in
M
runs into essentially the same issues as in the case of ordinary paths: to get a (smooth) superpath one requires agreement in a neighborhood of the gluing. Just as before, the problem goes away when one only considers suitably constant maps to
M
.

Definition 3.14
.

An
S
-family of
nearly constant super Euclidean paths in
M
is the data of a super Euclidean path
(\phi,(t_{\rm out},\theta_{\rm out}),(t_{\rm in},\theta_{\rm in}))
for which
\phi
is determined by a map
\phi_{0}
,

(76)

\displaystyle\phi\colon S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\xrightarrow{q}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}/{\mathbb{R}}\simeq S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\xrightarrow{\phi_{0}}M,

where the first arrow is the quotient of
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
by the
{\mathbb{R}}
-action on
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
.

Lemma 3.15
.

The groupoid of
S
-families of nearly constant super Euclidean path in
M
is equivalent to the discrete groupoid given by the value of the sheaf

(77)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)\coprod{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\leq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M),

on the supermanifold
S
.

Proof.

The follows the same argument as the proof of Lemma
3.13
, but the map
\phi
in (
75
) is required to be of the form (
76
) and hence determined by
\phi_{0}\colon S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\to M
.
∎

Lemma 3.16
.

Suppose that
\gamma=(\phi,(t_{\rm out},\theta_{\rm out}),(t_{\rm in},\theta_{\rm in}))
and
\gamma^{\prime}=(\phi^{\prime},(t_{\rm out}^{\prime},\theta_{\rm out}^{\prime}),(t_{\rm in}^{\prime},\theta_{\rm in}^{\prime}))
are
S
-families of positively (respectively, negatively) oriented nearly constant superpaths in
M
and that
\phi_{\rm out}=\phi_{\rm in}^{\prime}\colon S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\to M
. Then there is a well-defined concatenation
\gamma*\gamma^{\prime}
as a positively (respectively, negatively) oriented nearly constant superpath in
M
that is unique up to unique isomorphism.

Proof.

It suffices to work in the universal case (
77
), for which the concatenation of positively oriented superpaths is given by

\displaystyle({\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M))\times_{{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)}({\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M))

(78)

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m\times{{\rm id}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)

where
m
is the restriction of multiplication on
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
. The concatenation map in the negatively oriented case is completely analogous.
∎

Super Lie categories are reviewed in Definition
B.9
.

Definition 3.17
.

The super Lie category of
nearly constant superpaths in
M
, denoted
{\sf sPath}_{0}(M)
, has objects and morphisms,

{\rm Ob}({\sf sPath}_{0}(M))={\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)\coprod{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M),

{\rm Mor}({\sf sPath}_{0}(M))={\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)\coprod{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}^{1|1}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M),

where source, target, and unit data are given by the incoming and outgoing boundaries of a superpath and the identity superpath (using Lemma
3.15
) while composition is the concatenation of superpaths from Lemma
3.16
.

The above super Lie category can be understood as a subcategory of the following super Lie groupoid.

Lemma 3.18
.

There is an essentially surjective and faithful (but not full) functor

(79)

\displaystyle{\sf sPath}_{0}(M)\hookrightarrow\left({\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)\coprod{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)\right)/\!\!/{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1},

to the quotient super Lie groupoid for the
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
-action factoring through the homomorphism
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}
(see (
81
)), with the functor (
79
) determined by the standard inclusions
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0},{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\leq 0}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
.

Proof.

By definition, the source map on an
S
-point of
{\sf sPath}_{0}(M)
is

(\phi,(t_{\rm out},\theta_{\rm out}),(t_{\rm in},\theta_{\rm in}))\mapsto\phi_{\rm in},\qquad\phi_{\rm in}\colon S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\xhookrightarrow{i_{0}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\xrightarrow{\phi}M,

with
\phi
as in (
76
). Since
q\circ i_{0}={{\rm id}}_{S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}}
, we find
\phi_{\rm in}=\phi_{0}
in the notation of (
76
). The source map is therefore the projection
{\sf s}=p\colon{\rm Mor}({\sf sPath}_{0}(M))\to{\rm Ob}({\sf sPath}_{0}(M))
. The target map on an
S
-point of
{\sf sPath}_{0}(M)
is

(80)

\displaystyle(\phi,(t_{\rm out},\theta_{\rm out}),(t_{\rm in},\theta_{\rm in}))\mapsto\phi_{\rm out},\qquad\phi_{\rm out}\colon S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\xhookrightarrow{T_{t,\theta}\circ i_{0}}S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\xrightarrow{\phi}M.

Again using that
q\circ i_{0}={{\rm id}}_{S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}}
, the target map is the restriction of the (left) action

(81)

\displaystyle{\rm act}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)\to{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)

coming from the precomposition with the
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
-action on
{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}
,

{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},\qquad(t,\theta)\cdot\eta=(\theta+\eta),\quad(t,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}(S),\ \eta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}(S).

The lemma is proved.
∎

We recall the isomorphism of supermanifolds
{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)\simeq\Pi TM
leading to the isomorphism between functions and differential forms

(82)

\displaystyle C^{\infty}({\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M))\simeq C^{\infty}(\Pi TM_{\mathbb{C}})\simeq\Omega^{\bullet}(M).

Together with Lemma
3.18
, this will allow us to give explicit formulas for the structure maps in the super Lie category
{\sf sPath}_{0}(M)
, presented in terms of differential forms on
M
.

Remark 3.19
.

Using (
82
), functions on the quotient groupoid
{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)/\!\!/{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}
give a supergeometric interpretation of the de Rham complex of
M
, e.g., see
[
HKST11
, §3]
. Lemma
3.18
shows that the category of superpaths is closely related to this groupoid.

The isomorphism (
82
) implies
