determined by super parallel transport along nearly constant superpaths.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Proposition
1.4
by the existence of smooth index bundles in finite dimensions, e.g., see
[
BGV92
, §9.1]
.
∎

For an object
E\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
, let
\mathcal{H}\to M
denote the fiberwise Hilbert completion of the real vector bundle underlying
E
relative to the real inner product (see Proposition
1.10
);
\mathcal{H}
is typically only a continuous bundle on
M
.

Definition 7.5
.

Given
E\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
, the
\lambda
-cutoff theory

E_{<\lambda}\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(U_{\lambda})
is defined as the functor (
219
) applied to the super vector bundle
\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}\to U_{\lambda}
with Clifford linear self-adjoint connection
\nabla^{\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}}
, i.e.,

E_{<\lambda}:=E_{\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda},\nabla^{\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}}}\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(U_{\lambda})

in the notation of Corollary
7.4
.

Lemma 7.6
.

The partition function of
E
and the partition function of the
\lambda
-cutoff theory are related by

Z(E)|_{U_{\lambda}}=Z_{E^{<\lambda}}+d\eta_{\lambda}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(U_{\lambda};C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{>0})).

Proof.

This follows from the construction of the
\eta
-forms (
282
).
∎

7.2.
Cutoffs and the RG flow

The renormalization group flow interpolates between different cutoff theories, as the following lemma makes precise.

Proposition 7.7
.

The existence of cutoffs is preserved under the renormalization group flow: for
E\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
, the
\lambda
-cutoff of
{\sf RG}^{\mu}(E)
is canonically isomorphic to the
\mu\lambda
-cutoff of
E

(220)

\displaystyle{\sf RG}^{\mu}(E)_{<\lambda}\simeq E_{<\mu\lambda}\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(U_{\mu\lambda}).

Proof.

Using Proposition
7.7
, the description of the
{\sf RG}
-action in terms of Getzler rescaling allows us to analyze cutoffs. Unraveling Definition
1.11
, the
\mu\lambda
-cutoff for
E
is the same data as the
\lambda
-cutoff for
{\sf RG}^{\mu}(E)
: the cutoff bundles are the
same
subspace of
\mathcal{H}\to U_{\lambda}
with the
same
connection (note
{\mathbb{A}}_{1}
is invariant under the
{\sf RG}
-flow). Hence, there is indeed a canonical isomorphism (
220
), and furthermore
{\sf RG}^{\mu}(E)
satisfies properties (1) and (2) of Definition
1.11
. Property (3) follows from the Getzler rescaling description of the
{\sf RG}
-action, where the partition function of
{\sf RG}^{\mu}(E)
is computed by the Getzler-rescaled superconnection. Finally, by a change of variables one identifies the integral (
282
) for
E
and
\lambda\mu
with the integral for
{\sf RG}^{\mu}(E)
and
\lambda
. This completes the proof.
∎

Definition 7.8
.

An object
E\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)

flows to zero
under the renormalization group flow if for all
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
and compact submanifolds
K\subset M
, there exists
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that
{\sf RG}^{\mu}(E)|_{K}
has the zero theory as its
\lambda
-cutoff, i.e.,
K\subset U_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}|_{K}=\{0\}
.

Lemma 7.9
.

An object
E\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
flows to zero under the renormalization group flow if and only if the degree zero component of its associated superconnection
{\mathbb{A}}_{0}
is invertible.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the lemma when
M
is compact. Let
\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
be the minimum eigenvalue of
{\mathbb{A}}_{0}^{2}
. If
\nu>0
(i.e.,
{\mathbb{A}}_{0}
is invertible), for any
\lambda
we may choose some
\mu\gg 0
such that
\mu\nu>\lambda
. By Lemma
7.7
the
\lambda
-cutoff theory of
{\sf RG}^{\mu}(E)
is the zero theory. Conversely, if there exists some
\mu
such that
{\sf RG}^{\mu}(E)
has the zero bundle as its cutoff bundle, then
\mu\nu
must be larger than
\lambda
, and hence
\nu\neq 0
and
{\mathbb{A}}_{0}
is invertible.
∎

7.3.
Proof of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2

Definition 7.10
.

For
E\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
, choose a discrete subset
\Lambda\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that
\{U_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
is a locally finite cover. Then define the differential cocycle

(221)

\displaystyle{\rm Index}(E,\Lambda)=\{\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda},\nabla^{\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}},g_{\lambda\mu},e_{\lambda\mu},\eta_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\in\widehat{\rm KO}{}^{n}(M),

in the description of differential
{\rm KO}
-theory from
[
BE23b
, §7]
.

In brief, the formalism of
[
BE23b
, §7]
expands on the ideas of Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro to identify
(\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda},\nabla^{\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}})
with the local data of a differential
{\rm KO}
-class with Pontraygin form
{\sf sTr}_{{\rm Cl}_{n}}(e^{-(\nabla^{\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}})^{2}})
. The inclusions (
288
) and odd endomorphisms (
289
) provide the additional data to refine these local Pontryagin forms into a global Čech–de Rham cocycle representing the Pontryagin character of
{\rm Index}(E,\Lambda)
. The forms
\eta_{\lambda}
are a Čech–de Rham coboundary mediating between this cocycle and the global differential form
Z(E)
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

The index map (
221
) applied to the self-adjoint superconnection underlying
E\in 1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
gives a map of the desired type, but a priori depends on a choice of subset
\Lambda\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
determining a choice of locally finite subcover
\{U_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
. To show that the differential KO-class is independent of this choice, one applies the same argument that proves the differential families index is well-defined
[
BE23b
, §8.3]
. To review this argument, for a different choice
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
, we can compare the resulting
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-bundles on the mutual refinement
\{U_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in(\Lambda\bigcup\Lambda^{\prime})}
. For
\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}
, we obtain inclusions (
288
) of
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-bundles with commuting
{\rm Cl}_{-1}
-actions on the orthogonal complement to the inclusion. This gives the data of a stable equivalence of
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-bundles. By
[
BE23b
, Lemma 5.9]
, the
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-bundles are therefore concordant and the differential KO-classes agree. This shows that (
221
) is independent of the choice of
\Lambda
, and the index map is well-defined.

By Lemma
7.9
, objects in
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)
that are sent to zero under the RG flow are also sent to the zero class under the composition

1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)\to\widehat{\rm KO}{}^{n}(M)\to{\rm KO}^{n}(M),

since the degree zero part of their associated superconnection is invertible, and hence the underlying KO-class is trivial. This completes the proof. ∎

The following verifies that Theorem
1.1
is compatible with Fei Han’s construction of the Chern form of a
1|1
-Euclidean field theory built from Dumitrescu’s super parallel transport.

Proposition 7.11
.

The composition of the first two arrows

\displaystyle{\sf Vect}^{\mathbb{A}}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n},{\rm finite}}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm sPar}}}{{\to}}1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{n}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm Index}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widehat{\rm KO}{}^{n}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm curv}}}{{\to}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{\rm cl}(M)

sends a Clifford linear superconnection to the expected differential
{\rm KO}
-class, and hence the above composition sends a
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-linear superconnection
{\mathbb{A}}
to the differential form
{\sf sTr}_{{\rm Cl}_{n}}(e^{-{\mathbb{A}}^{2}})
representing its Chern character.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume
M
is compact. Choosing a cutoff valued
\lambda
larger than the maximum eigenvalue of
{\mathbb{A}}_{0}^{2}
, the index construction sends a vector bundle with Clifford linear superconnection to the associated differential class in
\widehat{\rm KO}{}^{n}(M)
, and the curvature of this class is as stated above. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Using Proposition
1.4
, for a family
\pi\colon X\to M
of Riemannian spin manifolds and
E\to X
a real vector bundle the
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-linear superconnection (
293
) acting on the Fréchet bundle
V=\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\times E)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
determines an object of
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{-n}(B)
via the representation of positively oriented superpaths,

\rho_{+}(t,\theta)=\exp(-t{\mathbb{A}}^{2}+\theta{\mathbb{A}}).

The pairing and reflection positivity data are inherited from the hermitian pairing on
\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\times E)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
. The real structure comes from complex conjugation on
\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\times E)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
. The standard properties of the Bismut superconnection reviewed in §
C.3
imply the existence of a differential index bundle, so we do indeed obtain an object in
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{-n}(M)
. Finally, applying the index map (
221
) to this object of
1|1\hbox{-{\sf eft}}^{-n}(B)
is precisely the (real) family Clifford index
[
BE23b
, Theorem 1.3]
. This completes the proof. ∎

Appendix A
Super hermitian structures,
*
-superalgebras, and supermanifolds

A.1.
Real structures and hermitian pairings

The category of
super vector spaces
is the category of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded vector spaces over
{\mathbb{C}}
equipped with the graded tensor product. The adjective “super” refers to the sign in the braiding isomorphism for this tensor product

\sigma\colon V\otimes W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}W\otimes V\qquad v\otimes w\mapsto(-1)^{|v||w|}w\otimes v\quad v\in V,\ w\in W

where
v
and
w
are homogeneous elements of degree
|v|,|w|\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2
.
Let
V^{\rm ev}\oplus V^{\rm odd}
denote the direct sum decomposition of
V
into its even and odd subspaces. The
grading involution
is the linear map
(-1)^{\sf F}\colon V\to V
that acts by
+1
on
V^{\rm ev}
and
-1
on
V^{\rm odd}
.

There is an involution on the category of super vector spaces that on objects reverses complex structures,
V\mapsto\overline{V}
, i.e.,
\overline{V}
is gotten from
V
by precomposing the action of
{\mathbb{C}}
with complex conjugation.

Definition A.1
.

A
real structure
on a super vector space
V
is an isomorphism
r\colon V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\overline{V}
of super vector spaces such that
\overline{r}\circ r={{\rm id}}_{V}
. The
real subspace
associated with a real structure is the fixed point set
V_{\mathbb{R}}\subset V
for
r
.

Definition A.2
.

A
hermitian form
on a super vector space
V
is the data of a map
\langle-,-\rangle\colon\overline{V}\otimes V\to{\mathbb{C}}
making the diagram commute,

(222)

\overline{V}\otimes V
{\mathbb{C}}
V\otimes\overline{V}
\overline{{\mathbb{C}}}
\langle-,-\rangle
\sigma
\overline{\langle-,-\rangle}
(\overline{\phantom{A}})

or equivalently, satisfying the formula
\langle x,y\rangle=(-1)^{|x||y|}\overline{\langle y,x\rangle}
for homogeneous elements. A hermitian form is
positive
if

(223)

\displaystyle\langle x,x\rangle>0,

\displaystyle x\neq 0\ {\rm even}\quad{\rm and}\quad i^{-1}\langle x,x\rangle>0,

\displaystyle x\neq 0\ {\rm odd.}

A
real
hermitian form on a super vector space with real structure is a hermitian form that when restricted to
(V_{\mathbb{R}})^{\rm ev}\subset V
takes values in
{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
and when restricted to
(V_{\mathbb{R}})^{\rm odd}\subset V
takes values in
i^{-1}{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
.

A positive hermitian form in the above sense determines a positive pairing
(-,-)
in the usual sense on the underlying (ungraded) vector space
V
by the formula

\displaystyle(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&|x|\neq|y|\\
\langle x,y\rangle&x,y\ {\rm even}\\
i\langle x,y\rangle&x,y\ {\rm odd}\end{array}\right.

with the property that the even and odd subspaces are orthogonal.
When
V
is endowed with a real structure and a real hermitian form, (
A.1
) determines a real inner product on
V_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Definition A.3
.

Let
V
be a super vector space equipped with a positive hermitian form
\langle-,-\rangle
. The
super adjoint
of a linear map
T\colon V\to V
is characterized by

(228)

\displaystyle\langle x,Ty\rangle=(-1)^{|x||T|}\langle T^{*}x,y\rangle.

In the translation to the ordinary pairing (
A.1
), we have

\displaystyle T^{*}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}T^{\dagger}&T\ {\rm even}\\
iT^{\dagger}&T\ {\rm odd}\end{array}\right.

where
(-)^{\dagger}
denotes the usual adjoint with respect to the positive pairing
(-,-)
.

A.2.
*
-superalgebras

A
super algebra
is an algebra object in super vector spaces. Super algebras are the objects of a monoidal category, where the tensor product of super algebras
A
and
B
is the super vector space
A\otimes B
with multiplication

(a\otimes b)\cdot(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})=(-1)^{|b||a^{\prime}|}aa^{\prime}\otimes bb^{\prime}.

The
opposite
of a super algebra
A
is a super algebra
A^{\sf{op}}
with the same underlying super vector space and multiplication

(232)

\displaystyle a\cdot_{{\sf{op}}}b:=(-1)^{|a||b|}b\cdot a

where
b\cdot a
is the multiplication in
A
. A
*
-superalgebra
is a super algebra together with a homomorphism
(-)^{*}\colon A\to\overline{A}{}^{\sf{op}}
that squares to the identity on
A
; we emphasize that an anti-homomorphism in the graded world carries a sign, and hence

(ab)^{*}=(-1)^{|a||b|}b^{*}a^{*}.

A
real structure
on a super algebra is an involutive isomorphism
r\colon A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\overline{A}
, and
A_{\mathbb{R}}\subset A
is the fixed subalgebra. Given a super algebra with real structure, a
*
-structure is
real
if
(-)^{*}
preserves the subalgebra
(A_{\mathbb{R}})^{\rm ev}\subset A
and sends the subspace
(A_{\mathbb{R}})^{\rm odd}\subset A
to
i\cdot(A_{\mathbb{R}})^{\rm odd}
.

Example A.4
.

Let
V
be a super vector space with hermitian inner product. Then the super algebra
{\rm End}(W)
has the structure of a
*
-superalgebra determined by the super adjoint (
228
). A real structure on
W
determines a real structure on
{\rm End}(W)
for which this
*
-structure is real.

Example A.5
.

The Clifford algebras
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}
defined in (
2.4
) have the structure of
*
-superalgebras using (
202
). Complex conjugation gives
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}
the structure of a real super algebra for which this
*
-structure is real.

A second notion of
*
-structure on a super algebra comes from endowing the underlying (ungraded) algebra with a
*
-structure; we refer to this as a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded
*
-algebra
. This is equivalent to an antilinear involution
(-)^{\dagger}
of
A
satisfying

(xy)^{\dagger}=y^{\dagger}x^{\dagger}.

One can pass back and forth between the two versions of
*
-structure by inserting some appropriate factors of
i

[
DEF+99
, page 90]
. We review this translation in the two examples of interest.

Example A.6
.

For a super vector space
V
with positive hermitian form
\langle-,-\rangle
, the super algebra
{\rm End}(V)
has the structure of a
*
-superalgebra via the super adjoint. It has the structure of a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded
*
-algebra via the adjoint with respect to the (ungraded) inner product determined by (
A.1
). These two notion of
*
-structure are related by (
A.1
).

Example A.7
.

In the case of the Clifford algebras
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}
, we obtain a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded
*
-algebra via the
{\mathbb{C}}
-antilinear extension of the map on generators,

(233)

\displaystyle f_{j}^{\dagger}=-f_{j},\qquad e_{j}^{\dagger}=e_{j}.

This differs from the
*
-superalgebra structure (
202
) by a factor of
i
.

A.3.
Modules over super
*
-algebras

Given a super algebra
A
, let
{}_{A}{\sf Mod}
and
{\sf Mod}_{A}
denote the categories of left and right
A
-modules, respectively, whose objects are super vector spaces with a (graded)
A
-action. These categories have symmetric monoidal structures from direct sum of
A
-modules. Similarly, let
{}_{A}{\sf Mod}_{B}
denote the category of
A-B
-bimodules. There are canonical equivalence of categories

(234)

\displaystyle{\sf Mod}_{A^{\sf{op}}\otimes B^{\sf{op}}}\simeq{}_{A}{\sf Mod}_{B^{{\sf{op}}}}\simeq{}_{A\otimes B}{\sf Mod}

where a left
A\otimes B
-bimodule
W
is sent to the
A-B^{\sf{op}}
-bimodule with the same underlying super vector space and action

a\cdot w\cdot b:=(-1)^{|w||b|}(a\otimes b)\cdot w

with similar formulas defining a right
A^{\sf{op}}\otimes B^{\sf{op}}
-module.
We often use notation like
{}_{A}W
for an object of
{}_{A}{\sf Mod}
, and
{}_{A}W_{B}
for an object of
{}_{A}{\sf Mod}_{B}
.

Example A.8
.

A
Clifford module
is a graded module over a Clifford algebra
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}
. The canonical isomorphism
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}^{\sf{op}}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}
leads to equivalences of categories

(235)

\displaystyle{}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-m}}{\sf Mod}\simeq{}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}}{\sf Mod}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{m}}\simeq{\sf Mod}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-n}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{m}}

which will allow us to translate any Clifford (bi)module into a left Clifford module.

Definition A.9
.

A module
V
over a
*
-superalgebra
A
is
self-adjoint
if the structure map
A\to{\rm End}(V)
is a
*
-homomorphism for a specified hermitian form on
V
.

Remark A.10
.

When
A={\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}
with the
*
-superalgebra structure (
202
), the above definition is equivalent to
[
BGV92
, Definition 3.3]
via the translation to
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded
*
-algebras in Examples
A.6
and
A.7
.

Example A.11
.

Define a left
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{1}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-1}
-module whose underlying super vector space is
{\mathbb{C}}^{1|1}
with action determined by

\displaystyle f\mapsto\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right],\qquad e\mapsto\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right].

The assignment (
A.11
) induces an isomorphism of super algebras,
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{1}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-1}\simeq{\rm End}({\mathbb{C}}^{1|1})
. In other words,
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{1}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-1}
is Morita equivalent to
{\mathbb{C}}
.
This
{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{1}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{-1}
-action is self-adjoint for the graded hermitian pairing on
{\mathbb{C}}^{1|1}
coming from the standard hermitian pairing on
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
under (
A.1
).

A.4.
Super traces

The
super trace
of an endomorphism
T\colon V\to V
is

(241)

\displaystyle{\sf sTr}(T):={\sf Tr}((-1)^{\sf F}\circ T)

where
{\sf Tr}
is the ordinary trace.
The
super commutator
of elements in a super algebra is

(242)

\displaystyle[a,b]=ab-(-1)^{|a||b|}ba\qquad a,b\in A.

A
super trace
on a super algebra is a map of super vector spaces

(243)

\displaystyle{\sf sTr}_{A}\colon A\to{\mathbb{C}},\qquad{\sf sTr}_{A}([a,b])=0

that vanishes on super commutators, and hence is determined by a linear map
A/[A,A]\to{\mathbb{C}}
.

A super trace on a super algebra
A
induces a super trace on the category of (finite-dimensional)
A
-modules. For right
A
-modules, this super trace comes from the composition

(244)

\displaystyle{\rm End}_{A}(W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}W\otimes_{A}W^{\vee}\to A/[A,A]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\sf sTr}_{A}}}{{\to}}{\mathbb{C}}

where
W^{\vee}:={\rm Hom}_{A}(W,A)
is the dual (left)
A
-module, and the map to
A/[A,A]
is the Hattori–Stallings trace induced by the evaluation pairing,

{\rm eval}\colon W\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}W^{\vee}\to A.

One obtains traces for bimodules (and left modules) from traces on
A\otimes B^{\sf{op}}
using (
234
).

Define the
Clifford super trace

(245)

\displaystyle{\sf sTr}_{{\rm Cl}_{n}}\colon{\rm End}(W)\to{\mathbb{C}},\qquad{\sf sTr}_{{\rm Cl}_{n}}(T):={\sf sTr}(\Gamma_{n}\circ T\colon W\to W)

where
\Gamma_{n}
is defined in (
6.6
).

A.5.
(Real) supermanifolds

Let
{\sf SMfld}
denote the category of supermanifolds with structure sheaves defined over
{\mathbb{C}}
; we refer to
[
ST11
, §4.1]
for a brief introduction and
[
DM99
]
for a more comprehensive one. We require a slight generalization of the usual definition that allows for supermanifolds with boundary. Analogously to the usual case, these are defined as Hausdorff, second countable locally ringed spaces that are locally isomorphic to
{\mathbb{H}}^{n|m}
, where
{\mathbb{H}}^{n|m}
is the restriction of the structure sheaf of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n|m}
to the half-space
{\mathbb{H}}^{n}:=\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\mid x_{1}\geq 0\}.

Throughout,
C^{\infty}
will denote the structure sheaf of a supermanifold
S
, which is a sheaf of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded complex algebras. Global sections are denoted
C^{\infty}(S)
. We refer to sections as
functions
on the supermanifold. Let
S_{{\rm{red}}}
denote the reduced manifold of
S
, i.e., the locally ringed space with the same underlying topological space and structure sheaf
C^{\infty}/{\rm nil}
where
{\rm nil}
is the sheaf of nilpotent functions, or equivalently, the sheaf of ideals generated by odd sections of
C^{\infty}
.

Example A.12
.

Let
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
denote the supermanifold whose underlying topological space is
{\mathbb{R}}
, and structure sheaf is the sheaf of sections of
\Lambda^{\bullet}\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\vee}
, where
\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}
is the trivial complex line bundle on
{\mathbb{R}}
. Then define
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\geq 0}
and
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}_{\leq 0}
by restricting the structure sheaf of
{\mathbb{R}}^{1|1}
to
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
and
{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
.

Example A.13
.

For a complex vector bundle
E\to N
over an ordinary manifold
N
, let
\Pi E
denote the supermanifold whose structure sheaf of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded algebras is given by

U\mapsto C^{\infty}(U):=\Gamma(U;\Lambda^{\bullet}E^{\vee}),\qquad U\subset N.

Every supermanifold
S
is isomorphic to
\Pi E
for some vector bundle
E\to N=S_{\rm red}

[
Bat79
]
.

In computations, we frequently identify a supermanifold with its functor of points, i.e., its image under the Yoneda embedding into presheaves (of sets) on
{\sf SMfld}
. We use the notation
N(S)
to denote the
S
-points of a supermanifold
N
.

Example A.14
.

The supermanifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{n|m}
is the locally ringed space with underlying topological space
({\mathbb{R}}^{n|m})_{\rm red}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and structure sheaf the sheaf of sections of
\Lambda^{\bullet}(\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m})^{\vee}
, the trivial rank
m
complex vector bundle on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. The
S
-points of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n|m}
are

{\mathbb{R}}^{n|m}(S)\simeq\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in C^{\infty}(S)^{\rm ev},\ \theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{m}\in C^{\infty}(S)^{\rm odd}\mid(f_{i})_{\rm red}=\overline{(f_{i})}_{\rm red}\in C^{\infty}(S_{\rm red})\}

where the reality condition on the
f_{i}
is imposed on the reduced manifold of
S
.

Many definitions in supermanifolds can be phrased in terms of the functor of points. For example, a
super Lie group
is a group object in supermanifolds.
Sometimes supermanifolds are first defined in terms of their functor of points and then proven to be representable.

Example A.15
.

Define
{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)
as the presheaf on supermanifolds whose value on
S
is the set of maps
S\times{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\to M
; this supermanifold is representable
{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)\simeq\Pi TM_{\mathbb{C}}
, e.g., see
[
HKST11
, Proposition 3.1]
. Consider the precomposition action of
{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}
on itself by translation,

{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}\times{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)\to{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M).

Differentiation at
0\in{\mathbb{R}}^{0|1}
gives an odd vector field on
{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)
. Under the isomorphism
C^{\infty}({\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M))\simeq C^{\infty}(\Pi TM_{\mathbb{C}})\simeq\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
, this vector field is the de Rham differential, acting on forms as an odd derivation.

Following the terminology from
[
HST10
,
ST11
]
, a
generalized supermanifold
is a presheaf on the site of supermanifolds (that typically is not representable). A map between generalized supermanifolds is a map between presheaves.

Example A.16
.

Given supermanifolds
\Sigma
and
X
, define the mapping object

{\sf Map}(\Sigma,X)\colon{\sf SMfld}^{\sf{op}}\to{\sf Set},\qquad{\sf Map}(\Sigma,X)(S):={\sf SMfld}(S\times\Sigma,X)

as a generalized supermanifold with the indicated
S
-points.

Example A.17

(
[
HST10
, Example 5.5]
)
.

Given a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded topological vector space
{\mathcal{V}}
, define a generalized supermanifold by the
S
-points

{\mathcal{V}}\colon{\sf SMfld}^{\sf{op}}\to{\sf Set},\qquad{\mathcal{V}}(S):=(C^{\infty}(S)\otimes{\mathcal{V}})^{\rm ev}

where the tensor product is the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded projective tensor product.

Definition A.18
.

There is an involution on the category of supermanifolds,

(246)

\displaystyle(\overline{\phantom{-}})\colon{\sf SMfld}\to{\sf SMfld},\qquad S\mapsto\overline{S}

sending a supermanifold to the same locally ringed space but whose sheaf has complex numbers acting through complex conjugation. A
real structure
on a supermanifold
S
is an isomorphism
r_{S}\colon S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\bar{S}
such that
\overline{r}\circ r={{\rm id}}_{S}
. A map
f\colon S^{\prime}\to S
between supermanifolds with real structure is
real
if
r_{S}\circ f=f\circ r_{S^{\prime}}
.

Example A.19
.

For a complex vector bundle
E\to N
, a real structure on
E
(as a vector bundle) determines a real structure on
\Pi E
(as a supermanifold)

C^{\infty}(\Pi E)\simeq\Gamma(N;\Lambda^{\bullet}E^{\vee})\simeq\Gamma(N;(\Lambda E_{\mathbb{R}}^{\vee})\otimes{\mathbb{C}})\qquad(E_{\mathbb{R}})\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\simeq E.

Indeed, complex conjugation determines a
{\mathbb{C}}
-antilinear map
C^{\infty}(\Pi E)\to C^{\infty}(\Pi E)
which provides an isomorphism of supermanifolds
\Pi E\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\overline{\Pi E}
.

Given a vector bundle
V\to M
, we obtain a vector bundle
p^{*}V\to{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)
by pulling back along the projection
p\colon{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)\simeq\Pi TM\to M
.

Lemma A.20
.

For any vector bundle
{\mathcal{V}}\to{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)
, there is an isomorphism
{\mathcal{V}}\simeq p^{*}V
where
V\to M
is a vector bundle on
M
.

Proof.

Let
i\colon M\hookrightarrow{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)
denote the inclusion of the reduced manifold. Given a vector bundle
{\mathcal{V}}\to{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)
, our candidate for
V\to M
in the statement is
V:=i^{*}{\mathcal{V}}
. Indeed, the composition
i\circ p
is homotopic to the identity on
{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)
. For example, consider the homotopy
{\mathbb{R}}\times\Pi TM_{\mathbb{C}}\to\Pi TM_{\mathbb{C}}
that rescales the odd fibers of
\Pi TM_{\mathbb{C}}\to M
by a scalar
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
; in the functor of points this map is
(t,x,\psi)\mapsto(x,t\psi)
for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}(S)
and
(x,\psi)\in\Pi TM_{\mathbb{C}}(S)\simeq{\sf Map}({\mathbb{R}}^{0|1},M)(S)
. Now we use that pullbacks along homotopic maps yield isomorphic vector bundles,
concluding that
p^{*}V=p^{*}i^{*}{\mathcal{V}}\simeq(i\circ p)^{*}{\mathcal{V}}
is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{V}}
.
∎

Appendix B
Review of Stolz and Teichner’s Euclidean field theories

In this section we review Stolz and Teichner’s definition of twisted geometric field theory following
[
ST11
]
. Their central construction is a
geometric bordism category
. This takes as input a
rigid geometry

(G,{\mathbb{M}})
and outputs a functor from smooth manifolds to categories internal to symmetric monoidal stacks on the site of supermanifolds,

(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(-)\colon{\sf Mfld}\to\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm Categories\ internal\ to}\\
{\rm symmetric\ monoidal\ stacks}\end{array}\right\},\qquad M\mapsto(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M),

where
(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)
is the
category of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-bordisms over
M
. Geometric field theories are then defined as functors out of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)
, and twisted field theories are certain natural transformations between functors out of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)
.

We also introduce
reflection positivity data
within this formalism (see §
B.5
), following the motivational discussion from §
2
. Although this structure is not part of Stolz and Teichner’s definition from
[
ST11
]
, it is implicit in their previous work
[
ST04
, Definition 2.1.1, Remark 2.1.2]
and seems to be
crucial for the
d=2
case of conjecture (
1
); see Remark
1.9
.

Remark B.1
.

The
d=2
case of conjecture (
1
) requires a generalization of the definitions below to fully-extended
2|1
-dimensional Euclidean field theories of degree
n
. In particular, this requires that one enhance the
2|1
-Euclidean bordism category to (some flavor of) bicategory that encodes cutting and gluing of
2|1
-Euclidean manifolds with corners. The framework for such a definition is a subject of current investigation, and likely to depart in various ways from prior approaches. Indeed, extending the definitions in
[
ST11
]
to bicategories internal to symmetric monoidal stacks seems rather unwieldy. At present, smooth bordism categories inspired by iterated Segal spaces seem more tractable, e.g., see
[
JFS17
,
DG20
,
LS21
,
BEP22
]
.

B.1.
Rigid geometries

Inspired by Thurston
[
Thu97
, Ch. 3.3]
, a
rigid geometry
is a triple
(G,{\mathbb{M}},{\mathbb{M}}^{c})
where
{\mathbb{M}}
is a supermanifold,
G
is a super Lie group acting on
{\mathbb{M}}
, and
{\mathbb{M}}^{c}\subset{\mathbb{M}}
is a codimension 1 submanifold. The supermanifold
{\mathbb{M}}
is the
local model
for the geometry, the group
G
is the
isometry group
, and
{\mathbb{M}}^{c}\subset{\mathbb{M}}
is a local model for (collared) boundaries. The data of a rigid geometry is often abbreviated to the pair
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
.

Definition B.2

(Sketch of
[
ST11
, Definitions 2.33 and 4.4]
)
.

A
family of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-supermanifolds
is data

(1)

a smooth submersion
p\colon Y\to S
;

(2)

a maximal atlas
\{U_{i}\}
of
Y
equipped with open embeddings
\varphi_{i}\colon U_{i}\hookrightarrow S\times{\mathbb{M}}
over
S
;

(3)

transition data
g_{ij}\colon p(U_{i}\bigcap U_{j})\to G
.

The embeddings
\varphi_{i}
are required to be compatible with transition data
g_{ij}
(using the
G
-action on
{\mathbb{M}}
) and the transition data must satisfy a further cocycle condition.

An
isometry
between
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-families is a pullback
f\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}
over a base change
S\to S^{\prime}
with the property that the restriction of
f
to the atlases determining the
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-structures is given by the action of
G
on open subspaces of
{\mathbb{M}}
. The collection of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-supermanifolds and isometries defines a stack
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-
{\sf SMfld}
on the site of supermanifolds; see
[
ST11
, §2.8]
and
[
BBES21
, Remark A.23]
.

A
family of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-pairs
is a
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-family
Y\to S
with a codimension 1 submanifold
Y^{c}\subset Y
over
S
with the property that in a chart for the
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-structure, the image of
U_{i}\bigcap Y^{c}\subset U_{i}
under
\varphi_{i}
lands in the subspace
S\times{\mathbb{M}}^{c}\subset S\times{\mathbb{M}}
. Isometries between
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-pairs are isometries of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-supermanifolds
Y\to Y^{\prime}
such that
Y^{c}\subset Y
is sent to
Y^{\prime c}\subset Y^{\prime}
. This determines a stack on the site of supermanifolds whose objects are
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-pairs.

We also refer to
[
HST10
, §6.3]
for additional discussion of rigid geometries.

Example B.3

(Euclidean rigid geometry)
.

Let
{\mathbb{A}}^{d}
be affine
d
-space, regarded as a Riemannian spin manifold. Its group of spin structure preserving isometries is
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d)
, where
{\rm Spin}(d)
acts on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
through the double cover
{\rm Spin}(d)\to{\rm SO}(d)
. The
d
-dimensional Euclidean geometry
is defined by the triple
(G,{\mathbb{M}},{\mathbb{M}}^{c})=({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d),{\mathbb{A}}^{d},{\mathbb{A}}^{d-1})
, where
{\mathbb{A}}^{d-1}\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{d}
comes from the standard inclusion of a totally geodesic hyperplane; after a choice of basepoint this is the standard inclusion
{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. The stack
({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rtimes{\rm Spin}(d),{\mathbb{A}}^{d})
-
{\sf SMfld}
of
d
-dimensional Euclidean manifolds has as objects fiber bundles
Y\to S
of
d
-manifolds endowed with flat Riemannian metrics and spin structures. The stack of
d
-dimensional Euclidean pairs has as objects fiber bundles
Y\to S
of
d
-manifolds endowed with flat Riemannian metrics and spin structures together with a family of totally geodesic codimension 1 submanifolds
Y^{c}\subset Y
over
S
.

Lemma B.4
.

Fix a rigid geometry
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
. Given a homomorphism
g\colon G\to G
and a
G
-equivariant map
f\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to{\mathbb{M}}
relative to
g
that preserves the image of the inclusion
{\mathbb{M}}^{c}\subset{\mathbb{M}}
, there is a uniquely determined map of stacks

F\colon(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-}{\sf SMfld}\to(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-}{\sf SMfld}.

Sketch of Proof.

The proof is the same argument as
[
HST10
, Lemma 6.19]
. Suppose we are given an
S
-family of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-manifolds
Y\to S
. We obtain a new
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-family
f(Y\to S)
given by the same fibration
Y\to S
, but where local charts
U_{i}\subset Y
are modified by postcomposition with
f
,

Y\supset U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{i}}}{{\hookrightarrow}}{\mathbb{M}}\times S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f\times{{\rm id}}_{S}}}{{\to}}{\mathbb{M}}\times S.

Because
f
is
G
-equivariant relative to
g
, the gluing data
g^{-1}g_{ij}g
determines a
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-structure on
Y
. Similarly, because
f
preserves the image of
{\mathbb{M}}^{c}\subset{\mathbb{M}}
, it determines an endofunctor on the category of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-pairs. Since the underlying map on the family
Y\to S
is the identity map, these endofunctors of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-manifolds determine maps of stacks.
∎

Definition B.5

(
[
HST10
, Definition 6.18]
)
.

A
real structure
on a rigid geometry
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
is a real structure on
{\mathbb{M}}^{c}
,
{\mathbb{M}}
and
G
such that all the maps defining the rigid geometry are real in the sense of Definition
A.18
.

B.2.
Categories internal to stacks

Definition B.6

(Sketch of
[
ST11
, Definition 2.13]
)
.

A
category
{\sf C}
internal to symmetric monoidal stacks
is the data of symmetric monoidal stacks
{\rm Ob}({\sf C})
and
{\rm Mor}({\sf C})
of objects and morphisms with source, target, unit, and composition functors

{\sf s},{\sf t}\colon{\rm Mor}({\sf C})\to{\rm Ob}({\sf C})\quad{\sf u}\colon{\rm Ob}({\sf C})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf C}),\quad{\sf m}\colon{\rm Mor}({\sf C})\times_{{\rm Ob}({\sf C})}{\rm Mor}({\sf C})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf C})

along with additional coherence data (e.g., associator and unitor) satisfying compatibility properties.

Definition B.7

(Sketch of
[
ST11
, Definition 2.18]
)
.

For
{\sf C}
,
{\sf D}
categories internal to symmetric monoidal stacks, an
internal functor

F=(F_{0},F_{1},\mu,\varepsilon)\colon{\sf C}\to{\sf D}
is defined as morphisms of stacks

,

, and 2-commuting data,

These data are required to satisfy further properties involving the associators and unitors.

Definition B.8

(Sketch of
[
ST11
, Definition 2.19]
)
.

An
internal natural transformation

(\eta,\rho)\colon F\Rightarrow G
between internal functors

is a morphism of stacks
\eta\colon{\rm Ob}({\sf C})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf D})
and 2-commuting data

as the the data of 2-morphism

between maps of stacks with respect to which
\rho
and

are compatible.

Definition B.9
.

A
super Lie category
is a category internal to supermanifolds whose source and target maps are submersions. For a super Lie category
{\sf C}
, let
{\rm Ob}({\sf C})
and
{\rm Mor}({\sf C})
denote the supermanifolds of objects and morphisms, and let

{\sf u}\colon{\rm Ob}({\sf C})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf C}),\ {\sf s},{\sf t},\colon{\rm Mor}({\sf C})\to{\rm Ob}({\sf C}),\ {\sf c}\colon{\rm Mor}({\sf C})\times_{{\rm Ob}({\sf C})}{\rm Mor}({\sf C})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf C})

denote the unit, source, target, and composition maps, respectively. A
smooth functor

F\colon{\sf C}\to{\sf D}
is a pair of maps of supermanifolds
F_{0}\colon{\rm Ob}({\sf C})\to{\rm Ob}({\sf D})
,
F_{1}\colon{\rm Mor}({\sf C})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf D})
satisfying the axioms for a functor between categories. A
smooth natural transformation

\eta\colon F\Rightarrow G
is a map of supermanifolds
\eta\colon{\rm Ob}({\sf C})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf D})
satisfying the axioms for a natural transformation between functors. Super Lie categories, smooth functors, and smooth natural transformations form a strict 2-category.

By regarding a supermanifold as a (representable) stack, any super Lie category determines a category internal to stacks (see Definition
B.6
). By the Yoneda lemma for stacks, functors and natural transformations between super Lie categories are equivalent to functors and natural transformations between their associated categories internal to stacks. This also allows us to consider functors from a super Lie category to a category internal to stacks.

Example B.10
.

Any supermanifold determines a discrete super Lie category, i.e., a super Lie category with only identity morphisms.

Remark B.11
.

Categories internal to stacks, internal functors, and internal natural transformations are not as well-behaved as one might hope. For example, an internal functor
{\sf C}\to{\sf D}
that is (appropriately) essentially surjective and fully faithful need not have an inverse functor. For example, let
{\sf C}
be the Čech groupoid of an open cover of
X
and
{\sf D}
be the (discrete) internal category determined by
X
; the evident internal functor
{\sf C}\to{\sf D}
has an inverse internal functor if and only if the cover admits a section. The “correct” framework for categories internal to stacks therefore requires a localization at an appropriate class of weak equivalences, analogous to the localization relating the 2-category of Lie groupoids with geometric stacks
[
Pro96
]
. For the manipulations in this paper, it suffices to work with categories internal to stacks before such a localization.

The following notions of internal involution and equivariance data expand on
[
HST10
, Definition 6.47]
and
[
ST11
, 2.6]
. The ambient involution
{\sf SMfld}\to{\sf SMfld}
is taken in the sense of
[
FH21
, Definition B.1]
, and in our applications will either be the identity involution or the conjugation functor (
246
) on supermanifolds.

Definition B.12
.

Fix an involution
{\sf SMfld}\to{\sf SMfld}
on the category of supermanifolds. For a category
{\sf C}

\tau\circ\tau\circ\tau\circ\tau
.

The following generalizes

to Stolz and Teichner’s internal categories.

Categories internal to stacks also have a notion of equivariance data for internal natural transformations, defined as follows.

Definition B.14
.

Let
({\sf C},\tau_{\sf C},\eta_{\sf C},\delta_{\sf C})
and
