B.3.
Stolz and Teichner’s geometric bordism categories

Given a smooth manifold
M
and rigid geometry
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
, we now recall the definition of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)
as a category internal to symmetric monoidal stacks.

Definition B.15

(
[
ST11
, Definitions 2.21, 2.46, end of 2.48, and 4.4]
)
.

Define a category
(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)
internal to symmetric monoidal stacks as follows.

An object of the stack
{\rm Ob}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M))
is an equivalence class of the data:

(1)

a
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-pair
Y^{c}\subset Y\to S
where
Y^{c}\to S
is proper;

(2)

a partition into two components
Y^{+}\coprod Y^{-}\simeq Y\setminus Y^{c}\subset Y
; and

(3)

a map of supermanifolds
\phi\colon Y\to M
.

The supermanifold
Y^{c}
is called the
core
. The supermanifold
Y\supset Y^{c}
in which the core sits is the
collar
. The collar is partitioned into an
outgoing
component
Y^{+}
and an
incoming
component
Y^{-}
.
We apply the following equivalence relation to these data:

(251)

\displaystyle(Y^{c}\subset Y,Y^{\pm},\phi)\sim(Y^{c}\subset Y,Y^{\pm},\phi^{\prime})\iff(\phi)|_{W^{+}\bigcup Y^{c}}=(\phi^{\prime})|_{W^{+}\bigcup Y^{c}}

where
W\subset Y
is an open submanifold containing
Y^{c}\subset Y
, and
W^{+}=W\bigcap Y^{+}
. In short, we only remember the data of the map
\phi
on a half-open germ of a neighborhood of
Y^{c}\subset Y
of the outgoing collar. We often abbreviate the data of an object of
{\rm Ob}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M))
to
\phi\colon Y\to M
.

A morphism in
{\rm Ob}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M))
, denoted
(Y^{c}_{0}\subset Y_{0},Y^{\pm}_{0},\phi_{0})\to(Y^{c}_{1}\subset Y_{1},Y^{\pm}_{1},\phi_{1})
is an equivalence class of open submanifolds
V_{0}\subset Y_{0}
and
V_{1}\subset Y_{1}
containing the cores and isometries
f\colon V_{0}\to V_{1}
in the commutative diagram

Y_{0}^{c}
Y_{1}^{c}
V_{0}
Y_{0}
V_{1}
Y_{1}
M
.
f
f|_{Y_{0}^{c}}
\phi_{0}
\phi_{1}

These data are subject to the equivalence relation that
f\colon V_{0}\to V_{1}
and
f^{\prime}\colon V_{0}^{\prime}\to V_{1}^{\prime}
represent the same isomorphism if
f|_{V_{0}\bigcap V_{0}^{\prime}}=f^{\prime}|_{V_{0}\bigcap V_{0}^{\prime}}
.

Disjoint union endows
{\rm Ob}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M))
with a symmetric monoidal structure.

An object of the stack
{\rm Mor}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M))
is given by the data:

(1)

a
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-family
\Sigma\to S
,

(2)

a map of supermanifolds
\Phi\colon\Sigma\to M
,

(3)

a pair of objects of
{\rm Ob}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M))
, denoted
\phi_{\rm in}\colon Y_{\rm in}\to M
,
\phi_{\rm out}\colon Y_{\rm out}\to M
,

(4)

spans of smooth maps
Y_{{\rm out}}\hookleftarrow W_{{\rm out}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{{\rm out}}}}{{\to}}\Sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{{\rm in}}}}{{\leftarrow}}W_{{\rm in}}\hookrightarrow Y_{{\rm in}}
, where
W_{{\rm out}/{\rm in}}\hookrightarrow Y_{{\rm out}/{\rm in}}
are open embeddings.

Define the notation

W^{\pm}_{\rm in/out}:=Y^{\pm}_{\rm in/out}\bigcap W_{\rm in/out},\quad i^{-}_{\rm in}=i_{\rm in}|_{W^{-}_{\rm in}},\quad i^{-}_{\rm out}=i_{\rm out}|_{W^{-}_{\rm out}}.

Then
\Sigma^{c}:=\Sigma\setminus(i_{\rm in}(W_{\rm in}^{-})\cup i_{\rm out}(W_{\rm out}^{+}))
is the
core
. The above data are require to satisfy

(1)

the image
W_{\rm in/out}\hookrightarrow Y_{\rm in/out}
contains a neighborhood of the core
Y^{c}_{\rm in/out}
;

(2)

\Sigma^{c}\to S
is proper;

(3)

i^{-}_{\rm in}
and
i^{-}_{\rm out}
are isometries onto their image in
\Sigma\setminus i_{\rm out}(W_{\rm out}^{+}\cup Y_{\rm out}^{c})
; and

(4)

the maps
\phi_{\rm in/out}\colon Y_{\rm in/out}\to M
are compatible with
\Phi\colon\Sigma\to M
.

Similar to (
251
), we impose an equivalence relation on objects

(252)

\displaystyle(\Sigma,Y_{\rm in},Y_{\rm out},\Phi_{1})\sim(\Sigma,Y_{\rm in},Y_{\rm out},\Phi_{2})\iff(\Phi_{1})|_{W^{+}_{\rm out}\bigcup\Sigma^{c}}=(\Phi_{2})|_{W^{+}_{\rm out}\bigcup\Sigma^{c}}

so again the equivalence class of
\Phi
is only the data of a half-open neighborhood of the core
\Sigma^{c}\to M
inside the outgoing collar.

A morphism in
{\rm Mor}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M))
is an equivalence classes of open sub supermanifolds
\Omega_{i}\subset\Sigma_{i}
,
V_{i,{\rm in}}\subset Y_{i,{\rm in}}
,
V_{i,{\rm out}}\subset Y_{i,{\rm out}}
satisfying

\Sigma_{i}^{c}\subset\Omega_{i}\subset\Sigma_{i},\quad Y_{i,{\rm in}}^{c}\subset V_{i,{\rm in}}\subset W_{i,{\rm in}}\bigcap i^{-1}_{\rm in}(\Omega_{i})\subset Y_{i,{\rm in}},\quad Y_{i,{\rm out}}^{c}\subset V_{i,{\rm out}}\subset W_{i,{\rm out}}\bigcap i^{-1}_{\rm out}(\Omega_{i})\subset Y_{i,{\rm out}}

and isometries
f_{\rm in}\colon V_{0,{\rm in}}\to V_{1,{\rm in}}
,
f_{\rm out}\colon V_{0,{\rm out}}\to V_{1,{\rm out}}
and
F\colon\Omega_{0}\to\Omega_{1}
in the commutative diagram of supermanifolds over
M

(253)

Y_{0,{\rm out}}^{c}
Y_{1,{\rm out}}^{c}
V_{0,{\rm out}}
\Omega_{0}
V_{1,{\rm out}}
\Omega_{1}
Y_{0,{\rm in}}^{c}
Y_{1,{\rm in}}^{c}
V_{0,{\rm in}}
V_{1,{\rm in}}
f_{\rm in}|_{Y_{0,{\rm out}}^{c}}
f_{\rm out}|_{Y_{0,{\rm in}}^{c}}
f_{\rm out}
f_{\rm in}
F

where we also require
F
preserves the core of the bordism,
F|_{\Sigma^{c}_{0}}\colon\Sigma^{c}_{0}\to\Sigma_{1}^{c}\subset\Omega_{1}
. These data are equivalent
(f,g,F)\sim(f^{\prime},g^{\prime},F^{\prime})
when

f_{\rm in}|_{V_{0,{\rm in}}\bigcap V_{0,{\rm in}}^{\prime}}=f^{\prime}_{\rm in}|_{V_{0,{\rm in}}\bigcap V_{0,{\rm in}}^{\prime}},\quad f_{\rm out}|_{V_{0,{\rm out}}\bigcap V_{0,{\rm out}}^{\prime}}=f^{\prime}_{\rm in}|_{V_{0,{\rm out}}\bigcap V_{0,{\rm out}}^{\prime}},\quad F|_{\Omega_{0}\bigcap\Omega_{0}^{\prime}}=F^{\prime}|_{\Omega_{0}\bigcap\Omega_{0}^{\prime}}.

Disjoint union endows
{\rm Mor}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M))
with a symmetric monoidal structure. We often abbreviate the data of an object of
{\rm Mor}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M))
to
Y_{\rm in}\coprod Y_{\rm out}\to\Sigma\xrightarrow{\Phi}M
, or even more succinctly to
\Phi\colon\Sigma\to M
.

Unit, source, and target functors
are given by the assignments on objects

{\sf u}(Y\to M)=(Y\coprod Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm fold}}}{{\to}}Y\to M),\qquad{\sf s}(Y_{\rm in}\coprod Y_{\rm out}\to\Sigma\to M)=(Y_{\rm in}\to M)

{\sf t}(Y_{\rm in}\coprod Y_{\rm out}\to\Sigma\to M)=(Y_{\rm out}\to M),

with similar assignments on morphisms. For any
S
, let
\emptyset\in{\rm Ob}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M))(S)
and
\emptyset\in{\rm Mor}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M))(S)
denote the empty object over
S
, i.e., the symmetric monoidal unit over
S
for these symmetric monoidal stacks.

Composition
is given by gluing bordisms along collars; see
[
ST11
, page 20]
and
[
HST10
, Definition 6.5]
.

We now address a small technical issue. The categorical foundations of
[
ST11
]
(following
[
MF06
]
) use strict fibered products of stacks in the definition of internal categories, see Definition
B.6
above. This framework leads to problems whenever one looks to replace an internal category with one whose objects and morphisms are equivalent (but not isomorphic) stacks. For example, generators and relations arguments involve such equivalences which are not isomorphisms.
A simple remedy to this technical issue is to modify Definition
B.6
, requiring the source and target functors to be fibrations of stacks. This implies that the strict fibered product agrees with the homotopy fibrered product.
Fortuitously, the source and target functors in
(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)
do indeed satisfy this property.

Lemma B.16
.

The source and target functors

{\sf s},{\sf t}\colon{\rm Mor}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M))\to{\rm Ob}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M))

are fibrations of stacks.

Proof.

We recall that a map of stacks is a fibration if it induces a fibration of groupoids over each supermanifold
S
, e.g., see
[
Hol08
]
. We prove the statement of the lemma for the functor
{\sf s}
; the case for
{\sf t}
is similar.
It suffices to show that for any isomorphism
f\colon{\sf s}(\Sigma_{0},\Phi_{0})\to(Y_{1},\phi_{1})
in
{\rm Ob}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M^{\prime}))(S)
where

(Y_{1},\phi_{1})\in{\rm Ob}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M^{\prime}))(S),\quad(\Sigma_{0},\Phi_{0})\in{\rm Mor}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M^{\prime}))(S),

there exists an isomorphism
(f,g,F)\colon(\Sigma_{0},\Phi_{0})\to(\Sigma_{1},\Phi_{1})
in
{\rm Mor}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M^{\prime}))(S)
with
{\sf s}(f,g,F)=f
. We recall that the notation
\Sigma_{0}
for a cobordism is an abbreviation for the data

(254)

\displaystyle(Y_{0,{\rm out}}\hookleftarrow W_{0,{\rm out}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{0,{\rm out}}}}{{\hookrightarrow}}\Sigma_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{0,{\rm in}}}}{{\hookleftarrow}}W_{0,{\rm in}}\hookrightarrow Y_{0,{\rm in}},\Phi_{0}\colon\Sigma_{0}\to M).

Similarly,
f\colon{\sf s}(\Sigma_{0},\Phi_{0})\to(Y_{1},\phi_{1})
is an abbreviation for the data of open sub supermanifolds
V_{0}\subset Y_{0}
,
V_{1}\subset Y_{1}
containing the core and an isometry
f\colon V_{0}\to V_{1}
preserving the cores.

Using the notation above, we define
(\Sigma_{1},\Phi_{1})
by the data

(255)

\displaystyle(Y_{0,{\rm out}}\hookleftarrow W_{0,{\rm out}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{0,{\rm out}}}}{{\hookrightarrow}}\Sigma_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{0,{\rm in}}\circ f^{-1}}}{{\hookleftarrow}}f(W_{0,{\rm in}}\bigcap V_{0})\hookrightarrow Y_{1},\Phi_{0}\colon\Sigma_{0}\to M).

where the relevant properties are inherited from (
254
).
Next define an isomorphism
(\Sigma_{0},\Phi_{0})\to(\Sigma_{1},\Phi_{1})
by taking
V_{0,{\rm out}}=V_{1,{\rm out}}=W_{{\rm out}}
,
\Omega_{0}=\Omega_{1}=\Sigma_{0}
,
V_{0,{\rm in}}=W_{0,{\rm in}}\bigcap V_{0}
,
V_{1,{\rm in}}=f(W_{0,{\rm in}}\bigcap V_{0})
,
f_{\rm out}={{\rm id}}_{W_{{\rm out}}}
,
F={{\rm id}}_{\Sigma_{0}}
and
f_{\rm in}=f|_{W_{0,{\rm in}}\bigcap V_{0}}
. By construction, the diagram (
253
) commutes with this data and the requisite properties are satisfied to define an isomorphism in
{\rm Mor}(1|1\hbox{-}{\sf EBord}(M))
. The source functor applied to this isomorphism agrees with
(f,V_{0},V_{1})
on restriction to
V_{0}\bigcap W_{0,{\rm in}}
and
f(V_{0}\bigcap W_{0,{\rm in}})
, so this morphism is equal to
f
because morphisms are defined as equivalence classes. This completes the proof.
∎

The following is evident from Definition
B.15
.

Lemma B.17
.

A map
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
of smooth manifolds induces an internal functor

f_{*}\colon(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)\to(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M^{\prime}),\qquad\begin{array}[]{ccc}(\phi\colon Y\to M)&\mapsto&(f\circ\phi\colon Y\to M^{\prime})\\
(\Phi\colon\Sigma\to M)&\mapsto&(f\circ\Phi\colon\Sigma\to M^{\prime})\end{array}

and these internal functors are compatible with composition of maps between manifolds.

B.4.
Twisted geometric field theories

Definition B.18

(Sketch of
[
ST11
, Definitions 2.24, 2.47, §4.3]
)
.

Define the category
{\sf{Vect}}
internal to symmetric monoidal stacks as follows. The stack
{\rm Ob}({\sf{Vect}})
classifies bundles of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded, nuclear Fréchet spaces over
{\mathbb{C}}
, i.e., locally free sheaves of nuclear Fréchet super vector spaces. The stack
{\rm Mor}({\sf{Vect}})
has objects maps between vector bundles, and as morphisms commuting squares

(256)

V
W
V^{\prime}
W^{\prime}
\simeq
\simeq

whose horizontal arrows are maps of vector bundles and whose vertical arrows are fiberwise isomorphisms of vector bundles, i.e., pullbacks along base changes. There are functors
{\sf s,t}\colon{\rm Mor}({\sf{Vect}})\to{\rm Ob}({\sf{Vect}})
from restricting to the left and right halves of the square (
256
). Finally, endow
{\rm Ob}({\sf{Vect}})
and
{\rm Mor}({\sf{Vect}})
with symmetric monoidal structures from the projective tensor product of topological vector bundles.

Remark B.19
.

The above deviates from the definition in
[
ST11
]
in two ways. First, we only work with vector bundles rather than general sheaves of topological vector spaces (i.e., we impose a locally free condition). Second, we specialize from general locally convex topological vector spaces to nuclear Fréchet spaces. We make this choice in order to guarantee that certain formal categorical structures in the functorial definition of quantum field theory agree with standard structures in physics: Fréchet spaces have the
approximation property

[
Sch71
, page 109]
, leading to a well-behaved theory for traces of nuclear operators, see
[
ST12
, §4.5]
. It also happens that all the desired constructions in index theory result in vector bundles of nuclear Fréchet spaces, so this restriction does not any exclude any of the desired examples. We refer to
[
Cos11a
, Appendix 2]
for a succinct overview of nuclear Fréchet spaces. Stolz and Teichner’s motivation to work with more general sheaves of topological vector spaces is explained in Remark
1.16
.

Definition B.20

(Sketch of
[
ST11
, Definition 5.1]
)
.

Define the
internal Morita category

{\sf{Alg}}
as a category internal to symmetric monoidal stacks as follows. The stack
{\rm Ob}({\sf{Alg}})
classifies locally trivial bundles of nuclear Fréchet super algebras and isomorphisms of algebra bundles. The stack
{\rm Mor}({\sf{Alg}})
classifies locally trivial bundles of nuclear Fréchet bimodules over topological algebras. Composition comes from the projective tensor product of bimodule bundles,

{\sf c}\colon{\rm Mor}({\sf{Alg}})\times_{{\rm Ob}({\sf{Alg}})}{\rm Mor}({\sf{Alg}})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf{Alg}}),\qquad{\sf c}(B,B^{\prime})=B\otimes_{A}B^{\prime}.

Viewing an algebra bundle as a bimodule over itself constructs the unit map
{\sf u}\colon{\rm Ob}({\sf{Alg}})\to{\rm Mor}({\sf{Alg}})
. Endow
{\rm Ob}({\sf{Alg}})
and
{\rm Mor}({\sf{Alg}})
with monoidal structures from the projective tensor product of algebra and bimodule bundles over the structure sheaf of the base.

Example B.21
.

Given any category
{\sf C}
internal to symmetric monoidal stacks, there is an internal functor

\mathbbold{1}\colon{\sf C}\to{\sf{Alg}}

defined as follows. For
Y\in{\rm Ob}({\sf C})(S)
, assign the trivial algebra bundle on
S
, i.e., the sheaf of algebras whose global sections are
C^{\infty}(S)
. For
\Sigma\in{\rm Mor}({\sf C})(S)
, assign the trivial bimodule bundle over
S
, i.e., the sheaf of bimodules whose global sections are
C^{\infty}(S)
as a bimodule over
C^{\infty}(S)
. The 2-commuting data
\mu
is determined by the canonical isomorphism
C^{\infty}(S)\otimes_{C^{\infty}(S)}C^{\infty}(S)\simeq C^{\infty}(S)
, while the 2-commuting data
\epsilon
comes from the canonical identification of the algebra
C^{\infty}(S)
with the identity bimodule over itself.

Definition B.22

(
[
ST11
, Definitions 2.25, 2.48, 4.12]
)
.

A
geometric field theory over
M
is an internal functor

(257)

\displaystyle E\colon(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)\to{\sf{Vect}}.

Definition B.23

(
[
ST11
, Definition 5.2]
)
.

A
twisted geometric field theory over
M
is an internal natural transformation
E

(258)

(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)
{\sf{Alg}}
E\Downarrow
\mathbbold{1}
\mathscr{T}

where
\mathscr{T}
is a
twist functor
and
\mathbbold{1}
is defined in Example
B.21
. Twisted field theories are the objects of a groupoid whose morphisms are isomorphisms between internal natural transformations.

Remark B.24
.

An (untwisted) geometric field theory in the sense of Definition
B.22
determines a twisted field theory in the sense of Definition
B.23
for the trivial twist functor
\mathscr{T}=\mathbbold{1}

[
ST11
, Lemma 5.7]
.

B.5.
Additional structures

Additional structures in the rigid geometry
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
lead to additional structures on the internal bordism category.

Lemma B.25
.

Fix a rigid geometry
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
. Given a homomorphism
g\colon G\to G
and a
G
-equivariant map
f\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to{\mathbb{M}}
relative to
g
that preserves the image of the inclusion
{\mathbb{M}}^{c}\subset{\mathbb{M}}
, there is a uniquely determined internal functor

F\colon(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)\to(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M).

Proof.

The endofunctors of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-manifolds from Lemma
B.4
determine maps of stacks,

F_{0}\colon{\rm Ob}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M))\to{\rm Ob}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)),\quad(Y\to S,\phi)\mapsto(f(Y\to S),\phi)

F_{1}\colon{\rm Mor}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M))\to{\rm Mor}((G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)),\quad(\Sigma\to M,\Phi)\mapsto(f(\Sigma\to M),\Phi),

by taking the same collar data on bordisms, but different
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-structures.
By construction, these maps of stacks are (strictly) compatible with source, target, unit, and composition functors defining
(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)
.
∎

Example B.26

(Bordism categories with flips)
.

In the context of Lemma
B.25
, suppose that
{\sf fl}\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to{\mathbb{M}}
is an involution commuting with the action of
G
and preserving
{\mathbb{M}}^{c}\subset{\mathbb{M}}
. This endows every family of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-manifolds with an involution. In practice, an involution
{\sf fl}\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to{\mathbb{M}}
comes from a
spin flip
automorphism, e.g., see
[
HST10
, §6.7, Lemma 6.39]
,
[
ST11
, Example 2.39 and §4.1]
and (
70
). Following
[
ST11
, §2.6]
, such an involution
{\sf fl}
allows one to regard the bordism category as a category internal to symmetric monoidal stacks with involution.

Next we observe that every super vector bundle or algebra bundle has a canonical automorphism coming from the parity involution on super vector spaces,

(259)

\displaystyle(-1)^{\sf F}\colon V\to V,\qquad(-1)^{\sf F}\colon A\to A

acting by the identity on even sections and
-1
on odd sections. This allows one to regard
{\sf{Vect}}
and
{\sf{Alg}}
as categories internal to symmetric monoidal stacks with involution. Stolz and Teichner incorporate these involutions to their field theories as follows.

Definition B.27

(
[
HST10
, Definition 6.44]
,
[
ST11
, §2.6]
)
.

Fix a rigid geometry
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
with an involution
{\sf fl}\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to{\mathbb{M}}
as in Example
B.26
. A
flip-preserving geometric field theory
is a functor (
257
) internal to the category of symmetric monoidal stacks with involution. Similarly, one defines
flip-preserving twists
and
flip-preserving twisted geometric field theories
as functors and natural transformations internal to symmetric monoidal stacks with involution.

Whenever a bordism category is endowed with a flip, we will always take (twisted) geometric field theories to mean
flip-preserving
(twisted) geometric field theories.

Example B.28

(Orientation reversal)
.

Fix an orientation on the manifold
{\mathbb{M}}_{\rm red}
, and suppose that
{\sf or}\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to{\mathbb{M}}
is a
G
-equivariant map with the property that
{\sf or}_{\rm red}\colon{\mathbb{M}}_{\rm red}\to{\mathbb{M}}_{\rm red}
is an orientation reversing map and
{\sf or}^{2}={\sf fl}
is the spin flip. Then Lemma
B.25
determines an
orientation reversal functor

(260)

\displaystyle{\sf Or}\colon(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)\to(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)

with a natural isomorphism
{\sf Or}\circ{\sf Or}\Rightarrow{{\rm id}}
(via the spin flip). Indeed,
{\sf Or}
determines an internal involution in the sense of Definition
B.12
.

In 1-dimensional field theories (i.e., quantum mechanics) orientation-reversal is the same as time-reversal, which is typically implemented on the space of states as a
{\mathbb{C}}
-antilinear map. This structure is part of
reflection positivity
, which can be adapted to functorial field theories by requiring equivariance data relative to actions generated by orientation reversal (
260
) and the functor on vector spaces coming from complex conjugation, e.g., see
[
FH21
, §3]
. However complex conjugation is more subtle in the context of supermanifolds, as we explain presently. There are conjugation functors,

(261)

\displaystyle(\overline{\phantom{-}})\colon{\sf{Vect}}\to{\sf{Vect}},\ \ (V\to S)\mapsto(\overline{V}\to\overline{S})\qquad(\overline{\phantom{-}})\colon{\sf{Alg}}\to{\sf{Alg}},\ \ (A\to S)\mapsto(\overline{A}\to\overline{S})

sending a vector bundle, algebra bundle, etc., to the same bundle but where the complex numbers act through conjugation; when the input is a bundle over a supermanifold
S
, the output is a bundle over the conjugate supermanifold,
\bar{S}
(see Definition
A.18
). In other words, the natural internal involution of
{\sf{Alg}}
(see Definition
B.12
) covers the conjugation functor on supermanifolds. For this reason it does not make sense to ask for equivariance data mixing (
260
) and (
261
), as they are internal functors covering different functors on supermanifolds (
{\sf Or}
covers the identify functor). This is repaired by demanding a real structure on the relevant bordism category, which can be gotten from a real structure on the rigid geometry in the sense of Definition
B.5
.

Example B.29

(Bordism categories with real structures,
[
HST10
, §8]
)
.

Given an
S
-family of
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-supermanifolds
(Y\to S)
, a real structure on the rigid geometry
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
gives a unique
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
-stucture for the
\overline{S}
-family
(\overline{Y}\to\overline{S})
. This extends to an internal functor

(262)

\displaystyle{\sf R}\colon(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)\to(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M),\qquad(S\leftarrow Y\to M)\mapsto(\overline{S}\leftarrow\overline{Y}\to\overline{M}\simeq M).

The identification
\overline{M}\simeq M
uses that
M
is an ordinary manifold, and hence has a canonical real structure when regarded as a supermanifold. Via the forgetful functor
(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)\to{\sf SMfld}
that sends an
S
-family of bordisms to
S
, the internal functor (
262
) covers the conjugation functor on supermanifolds. We observe that
{\sf R}
determines an internal involution in the sense of Definition
B.12
.

The functor
\mathbbold{1}\colon(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}\to{\sf{Alg}}
has canonical equivariance data for any internal involution on the source.

Definition B.30
.

Let
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
be a rigid geometry with a chosen real structure and orientation reversing automorphism.
A
reflection structure
for a twist
\mathscr{T}
is equivariance data for the involutions
{\sf R}\circ{\sf Or}
and
(\overline{\phantom{-}})
.
A
reflection structure
for a twisted field theory
E\colon\mathbbold{1}\to\mathscr{T}
is equivariance data for the internal natural transformation
E
.

Remark B.31
.

Roughly, a reflection structure stipulates that orientation reversal of bordisms correspond to complex conjugation of vector spaces (which is data). In tandem with this structure is the
reflection positivity property
, which demands that a certain hermitian pairing is positive. Suggestively we write this pairing as

(263)

\displaystyle\overline{E(Y)}\otimes E(Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h\otimes{{\rm id}}}}{{\simeq}}E(\overline{Y})\otimes E(Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E(\Sigma)}}{{\to}}{\mathbb{C}}

where the isomorphism
h
comes from the reflection structure, and
\Sigma
is a bordism with boundary
\overline{Y}\coprod Y
where
Y
is the orientation reversal of
Y
. Then the positivity property is the positivity of the pairing (
263
).
We provide the precise property for
1|1
-Euclidean field theories in Definition
5.16
.

Definition B.32

(Compare
[
HST10
, Definition 6.47]
)
.

Let
(G,{\mathbb{M}})
be a rigid geometry with a chosen real structure, and
\mathscr{T}\colon(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)\to{\sf{Alg}}
. A
real structure
for
\mathscr{T}
is equivariance data for the involutions
{\sf R}
and
(\overline{\phantom{-}})
. Given a twisted field theory
E\colon\mathbbold{1}\to\mathscr{T}
, a
real structure
on
E
is equivariance data for the internal natural transformation.

Example B.33

(Renormalization group flow)
.

In many cases, there is a nontrivial
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
-action on
{\mathbb{M}}
that commutes with the
G
-action. For example, the Euclidean rigid geometries from Example
B.3
admit such a structure from the standard dilation action on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Applying Lemma
B.25
, this leads to the action of the
renormalization group
,

(264)

\displaystyle{\sf RG}^{\mu}\colon(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)\to(G,{\mathbb{M}})\hbox{-{\sf Bord}}(M)\qquad\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0},

which then acts on the category of (twisted) field theories by precomposition with the internal functor (
264
).

Appendix C
The families index in KO-theory

C.1.
Superconnections

A super vector bundle is taken to be a locally free sheaf of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded nuclear Fréchet spaces; we make no finite rank assumption.

Definition C.1

(
[
Qui85
]
)
.

Let
V\to M
be a complex vector bundle. A
superconnection

{\mathbb{A}}
on
V
is an odd,
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear map of Fréchet spaces satisfying a Leibniz rule

(265)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(M;V)\to\Omega^{\bullet}(M;V),\quad{\mathbb{A}}(\alpha\cdot v)=d\alpha\cdot v+(-1)^{|\alpha|}\alpha\cdot{\mathbb{A}}v

where
v\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;V)
and
\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
.

Using the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading on forms, a superconnection can be written as

(266)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}=\sum{\mathbb{A}}^{[k]},\qquad{\mathbb{A}}^{[k]}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(M;V)\to\Omega^{\bullet+k}(M;V)

where
{\mathbb{A}}_{1}=\nabla
is an ordinary connection on
V
, and
{\mathbb{A}}_{2k}\in\Omega^{2k}(M;{\rm End}(V)^{{\rm odd}})
,
{\mathbb{A}}_{2k+1}\in\Omega^{2k+1}(M;{\rm End}(V)^{{\rm ev}})
are endomorphism valued forms.

Definition C.2
.

Suppose that
\langle-,-\rangle\colon\overline{V}\otimes V\to\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}
is a (super) hermitian pairing, where
\otimes
is the projective tensor product. Then a superconnection
{\mathbb{A}}
on
V
is
self-adjoint
if

(267)

\displaystyle\langle\overline{{\mathbb{A}}}_{1}x,y\rangle+(-1)^{|x||y|}\langle x,{\mathbb{A}}_{1}y\rangle=d\langle x,y\rangle,\qquad{\mathbb{A}}_{k}^{*}=i^{k+1}\overline{{\mathbb{A}}}_{k},

where
{\mathbb{A}}_{k}^{*}
is the super adjoint of
{\mathbb{A}}_{k}
.

Remark C.3
.

Translating from super adjoints to ordinary adjoints as in (
A.1
), the above is equivalent to the standard definition of self-adjoint superconnection
[
BGV92
, page 117]
.

For a superconnection
{\mathbb{A}}
, define the 1-parameter family,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}(u):=u^{-1/2}{\mathbb{A}}^{[0]}+{\mathbb{A}}^{[1]}+u^{1/2}{\mathbb{A}}^{[2]}+\dots+u^{j/2}{\mathbb{A}}^{[j]}+\dots\quad

Definition C.4
.

The
Chern form
of a
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-linear superconnnection is the Clifford super trace (which need not exist for infinite rank bundles)

(268)

\displaystyle{\rm Ch}({\mathbb{A}}):={\sf sTr}_{{\mathbb{C}}{\rm l}_{n}}(e^{-u{\mathbb{A}}(u)^{2}})\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;{\mathbb{C}}[u^{\pm 2}])).

The absence of fractional powers of
u
in
{\rm Ch}({\mathbb{A}})
follows from the fact that the super trace of an odd endomorphism is zero. The self-adjoint property further implies that the trace only has even powers of
u
.

Lemma C.5
.

When it exists, the Chern form of a
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-linear superconnection
{\mathbb{A}}
is closed and has total degree
n
.

C.2.
Clifford modules and KO-theory

We overview a model for the KO-spectrum due to
[
Che07
, §1.3-1.4]
and
[
HST10
, Theorem 7.1]
, see also
[
BE23b
, §4]
.

Definition C.6

(
[
Seg68
, §2]
)
.

A
topological category
is a category in which the objects and morphisms are topological spaces for which the structure maps (source, target, unit, composition) are continuous. For a topological category
\mathcal{C}
, let
\mathcal{C}_{0}
denote the space of objects and
\mathcal{C}_{1}
the space of morphisms.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{n}
denote an infinite-dimensional
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded separable Hilbert space with a self-adjoint action by
{\rm Cl}_{n}
with the property that every irreducible representation of
{\rm Cl}_{n}
appears with infinite multiplicity in
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Let
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
denote the algebra of
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-linear bounded operators in the norm topology.

Definition C.7
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{n}
be the category whose objects are finite-dimensional
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-submodules
W\subset\mathcal{H}_{n}
and whose morphisms are

\displaystyle{\rm Mor}(W_{0},W_{1}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\begin{array}[]{c}{\rm Cl}_{n}\otimes{\rm Cl}_{-1}-{\rm action\ on\ }W_{0}^{\perp}\\
{\rm extending\ the}\ {\rm Cl}_{n}{\rm-action}\end{array}&{\rm if}\ W_{0}\subseteq W_{1}\subset\mathcal{H}_{n}\\
\hbox{}\\
\emptyset&{\rm else}\end{array}\right.

where
W_{0}^{\perp}\subseteq W_{1}
denotes the orthogonal complement of the subspace
W_{0}\subseteq W_{1}
. Composition for a nested inclusion
W_{0}\subseteq W_{1}\subseteq W_{2}
uses the direct sum of Clifford modules to obtain a
{\rm Cl}_{n}\otimes{\rm Cl}_{-1}
-module structure on the orthogonal complement of
W_{0}\subseteq W_{2}
. Endow the objects and morphisms of
\mathcal{M}_{n}
with the subspace topology for the embeddings

(275)

\displaystyle{\rm Obj}(\mathcal{M}_{n})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n}),\qquad{\rm Mor}(\mathcal{M}_{n})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})^{\times 3}

where the first inclusion identifies a submodule
W_{0}\subset\mathcal{H}_{n}
with the (finite-rank) projection
\mathcal{H}_{n}\to W_{0}
. For the second inclusion, a triple
(W_{0},W_{1},e)
determines a pair of finite rank projection operators and a bounded operator
e
.

Definition C.8
.

The
nerve
of a topological category
\mathcal{C}
is the simplicial space whose
k
-simplices are
N_{k}\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{k}
for
k=0,1
, and for
k>1
are length
k
chains of composable morphisms

\displaystyle N_{k}\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{x_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{1}}}{{\to}}x_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{2}}}{{\to}}x_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{3}}}{{\to}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{k}}}{{\to}}x_{k}\mid x_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{0},f_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{1}\times_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}\cdots\times_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}\mathcal{C}_{1},

topologized as the fibered product.
The face maps in
N_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}
are determined by composing morphisms, and degeneracies are determined by the unit map in
\mathcal{C}
.

The
geometric realization
of a simplicial space
Z_{\bullet}
is

(276)

\displaystyle|Z_{\bullet}|:=(\coprod_{n}Z_{n}\times\Delta^{n})/{\sim}\qquad(f^{*}x,t)\sim(x,f_{*}t)\quad\forall f\colon[l]\to[k]

where
\Delta^{n}
is the standard
n
-simplex, and
f\colon[l]\to[k]
is an order-preserving map. Geometric realization is natural: a map of simplicial spaces
F\colon Z_{\bullet}\to Y_{\bullet}
determines a map between realizations,
|F|\colon|Z_{\bullet}|\to|Y_{\bullet}|
.

Proposition C.9

(
[
HST10
, Theorem 7.1]
,
[
Che07
, §1.3-1.4]
)
.

The classifying space
{\rm B}\mathcal{M}_{n}
represents the functor
{\rm KO}^{n}(-)
.

Given a topological space
Z
and an open cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
Z
, define
U_{\sigma}=\bigcap_{i\in\sigma}U_{i}
as the intersection for
\sigma\subset I
a nonempty finite subset.

Definition C.10
.

For
Z
a topological space and
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
an open cover, the
Čech category
, denoted
\check{{\rm C}}(U_{i})
, is the topological category whose objects are
\check{{\rm C}}(U_{i})_{0}=\coprod_{\sigma}U_{\sigma}
and whose morphisms are the space
\check{{\rm C}}(U_{i})_{1}=\coprod_{\sigma\subseteq\tau}U_{\tau}
. The source and target maps are determined by the projection and inclusion
U_{\tau}\subseteq U_{\sigma}
, the unit is induced by the identity map
U_{\sigma}\to U_{\sigma}
, and composition comes from nested inclusions.

Lemma C.11

(
[
Seg68
, Proposition 4.12]
,
[
DI04
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

For
Z
a topological space and
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
an open cover, the canonical map

(277)

\displaystyle{\rm B}\check{C}(U_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}Z,

is a homotopy equivalence.

Definition C.12

(
[
BE23b
, §4]
)
.

A
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-bundle
on a manifold
M
is data

(1)

an open cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
M
,

(2)

for each finite subset
\sigma\subset I
, a metrized super vector bundle
W_{\sigma}\to U_{\sigma}
with
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-action such that the fibers of
W_{\sigma}
are self-adjoint
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-modules;

(3)

for each
\sigma\subset\tau
, a
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-equivariant, injective map of super vector bundles
g_{\sigma\tau}\colon W_{\tau}\hookrightarrow W_{\sigma}
, together with a
{\rm Cl}_{n}\otimes{\rm Cl}_{-1}
-action on
W_{\tau}^{\perp}\subset W_{\sigma}
on the orthogonal complement of the image extending the
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-action.

For
\sigma\subset\tau\subset\rho
, we require an equality of maps
g_{\sigma\tau}\circ g_{\tau\rho}=g_{\sigma\rho}\colon W_{\rho}\hookrightarrow W_{\sigma}
, and that the
{\rm Cl}_{-1}
-action on
W_{\rho}^{\perp}\subset W_{\tau}\hookrightarrow W_{\sigma}
restricts from the
{\rm Cl}_{-1}
-action on
W_{\tau}^{\perp}\subset W_{\sigma}
.

Corollary C.13
.

Given a
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-bundle on
M
, there exists a continuous functor

(278)

\displaystyle\check{{\rm C}}(U_{i})\to\mathcal{M}_{n}

that is unique up to a contractible space of choices. After realization, this determines a map
M\to B\mathcal{M}_{n}\simeq{\rm KO}^{n}
into the
n
th space of
{\rm KO}
spectrum.

Proof.

The functor (
278
) comes from choosing a
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-equivariant embedding
W_{\sigma}\subset\mathcal{H}_{n}\times U_{\sigma}
over
U_{\sigma}
for each
\sigma\subset I
. By Kuiper’s theorem
[
Kui65
]
, the space of such choices is contractible. Then the corollary follows from Proposition
C.9
and Lemma
C.11
.
∎

Lemma C.14

(
[
BE23b
, §6]
)
.

Given a
{\rm Cl}_{n}
bundle on
M
, choices of self-adjoint
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-linear superconnections
{\mathbb{A}}_{\sigma}
on each
W_{\sigma}
uniquely determine a Čech–de Rham representative of the Pontryagin character
{\rm Ch}(W)\in{\rm H}^{\bullet}(M;{\mathbb{R}}[u,u^{-1}])
of
[W]=[\{U_{\sigma},W_{\sigma},g_{\sigma\tau},e_{\sigma\tau}\}]\in{\rm KO}^{n}(M)
.

Sketch of proof.

Over each
U_{i}
, a choice of superconnection on
W_{i}
gives a differential form
{\rm Ch}_{n}({\mathbb{A}}_{i})\in\Omega^{\bullet}(U_{i};{\mathbb{R}}[u,u^{-1}])
of total degree
n
. The data
g_{\sigma\tau},e_{\sigma\tau}
allow one to extend this to a Čech–de Rham cocycle.
∎

Definition C.15
.

A
differential refinement
of a
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-bundle
V=\{V_{\sigma},g_{\sigma\tau},e_{\sigma\tau}\}
defined relative to an open cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is data

i.

for each
\sigma\subset I
, a self-adjoint
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-linear superconnection
{\mathbb{A}}_{\sigma}
on
V_{\sigma}\to U_{\sigma}
, and

ii.

a closed global form
{\rm Ch}(V,{\mathbb{A}},\phi)\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;{\mathbb{R}}[\alpha^{\pm 1}])
of total degree
n
;

iii.

a coboundary
\{\phi_{\sigma}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(U_{\sigma};{\mathbb{R}}[\alpha^{\pm 1}])\}
between
{\rm Ch}(V,{\mathbb{A}},\phi)
and the Čech–de Rham cocycle
{\rm Ch}_{n}(\{U_{i}\},{\mathbb{A}}_{i})\in\Omega^{\bullet}(U_{*};{\mathbb{R}}[u^{\pm 1}])
in Lemma
C.14
.

A differential refinement of a
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-bundle is a
differential cocycle
denoted by
(V,{\mathbb{A}},\phi)=\{(V_{\sigma},{\mathbb{A}}_{\sigma},\phi_{\sigma}),(g_{\sigma\tau},e_{\sigma\tau})\}
.

C.3.
Index bundles

Given a self-adjoint,
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-linear superconnection
{\mathbb{A}}
on a vector bundle
V\to M
, define the subset
U_{\lambda}\subset M
for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
as follows. A point
m\in M
belongs to
U_{\lambda}
if
\lambda
is not an eigenvalue of the (non-negative) operator
({\mathbb{A}}^{[0]})^{2}_{m}
(using the notation (
266
)):

(279)

\displaystyle U_{\lambda}=:\{m\in M\mid\lambda\notin{\rm Spec}(({\mathbb{A}}^{[0]})^{2}_{m})\}\subset M.

Let
\mathcal{H}\to M
denote the fiberwise Hilbert completion of the metrized vector bundle
V\to M
; typically
\mathcal{H}\to M
is only a continuous (rather than smooth) vector bundle. Over each subset
U_{\lambda}\subset M
, define a subset
\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}\subset\mathcal{H}|_{U_{\lambda}}
as the direct sum of
\nu
-eigenspaces
\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\nu}
of
({\mathbb{A}}^{[0]})^{2}_{m}
with eigenvalue
\nu<\lambda

(280)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}_{m}:=\bigoplus_{\nu<\lambda}\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\nu}\subset\mathcal{H}_{m}

Letting
m
vary,
\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}
denotes the
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-invariant subset of
\mathcal{H}|_{U_{\lambda}}
whose fibers are
\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}_{m}
. Define the maps (of sets) over
U_{\lambda}

(281)

\displaystyle p^{\lambda}\colon\mathcal{H}|_{U_{\lambda}}\to\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda},\qquad i_{\lambda}\colon\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}\to\mathcal{H}|_{U_{\lambda}}

where
p^{\lambda}
is the fiberwise orthogonal projection, and
i_{\lambda}
is the fiberwise inclusion.

Definition C.16
.

A self-adjoint Clifford linear superconnection
{\mathbb{A}}

admits smooth index bundles
if

(1)

the collection of subsets
\{U_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}}
defined in (
279
) form a smooth open cover;

(2)

the subsets
\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}\subset\mathcal{H}|_{U_{\lambda}}\to U_{\lambda}
are smooth vector bundles;

(3)

the composition defines a smooth connection:

\nabla^{<\lambda}:=p^{\lambda}\circ{\mathbb{A}}^{[1]}|_{U_{\lambda}}\circ p^{\lambda}\colon\Gamma(\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda})\to\Omega^{1}(U_{\lambda};\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}),

(4)

the limit of Chern–Simons forms

(282)

\displaystyle\eta_{\lambda}(t):=\lim_{r\to\infty}\int_{1}^{r}{\sf sTr}_{{\rm Cl}_{n}}(\frac{d{\mathbb{A}}_{\lambda}(s)}{ds}\exp({\mathbb{A}}_{\lambda}(s)))ds\in\Omega^{\bullet}(U_{\lambda};C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{>0})[u^{\pm 1}])

exists and satisfies

(283)

\displaystyle d\eta_{\lambda}(t)={\rm Ch}({\mathbb{A}}(t))|_{U_{\lambda}}-{\rm Ch}(\nabla^{<\lambda})

where in (
282
),
{\mathbb{A}}_{\lambda}(s)
is the 1-parameter family of superconnnections on
V|_{U_{\lambda}}\oplus\Pi\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}_{\lambda}(s)={\mathbb{A}}_{\lambda}(s)^{[0]}+{\mathbb{A}}^{[1]}\oplus\nabla^{<\lambda}+\sum_{i>1}s^{-i}{\mathbb{A}}^{[i]}\oplus 0_{\Pi\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}},\quad{\mathbb{A}}_{\lambda}(s)^{[0]}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}{\mathbb{A}}^{[0]}&s\cdot i_{\lambda}\\
s\cdot p^{\lambda}&0\end{array}\right]

where
i_{\lambda}
and
p^{\lambda}
are the maps (
281
).

We sketch the proof of the equality (
283
). At
s=0
, the superconnection is a direct sum of the Bismut superconnection and the (parity reversal of the) ordinary connection
\nabla^{<\lambda}
, giving the right hand side of (
283
). For
s>0
, the operator
{\mathbb{A}}_{\lambda}(s)^{[0]}
is invertible
[
FL10
, Lemma 7.18]
, and so its Chern character is zero; the limit (
282
) gives an explicit coboundary for the Chern form.

For a superconnection admitting cutoffs, we review the index bundle construction from
[
BE23b
, §8.2-8.3]
. We note that to construct an index map to KO-theory, only (1) and (2) are necessary in Definition
C.16
; conditions (3) and (4) allow a refinement to differential KO-theory.

Proposition C.17
.

Given a
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-linear superconnection admitting cutoffs, a choice of locally finite open cover
\{U_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
associated with a discrete subset
\Lambda\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
determines a map

(287)

\displaystyle{\rm Ind}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{A}})\colon M\to{\rm KO}^{n},

to the
n
th space in the
{\rm KO}
-spectrum that is unique up to contractible choice. Given choices
\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
related by a refinement of open covers, there is a unique homotopy between the associated maps (
287
).

Proof.

The
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-modules
\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}\to U_{\lambda}
determine class
[\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}]\in{\rm KO}^{n}(U_{\lambda})
for each
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
. On overlaps
U_{\lambda}\bigcap U_{\mu}
for
\lambda<\mu
there are canonical inclusions

(288)

\displaystyle g_{\lambda\mu}\colon\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{<\mu}

and the orthogonal complement to the image of
g_{\lambda\mu}
has a
{\rm Cl}_{n}\otimes{\rm Cl}_{-1}
-action extending the
{\rm Cl}_{n}
-action, determined by the invertible odd endomorphisms

(289)

\displaystyle e^{\lambda\mu}|_{\mathcal{H}_{b}^{\nu}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\nu}}{\mathbb{A}}^{[0]}|_{\mathcal{H}_{b}^{\nu}},\quad\mathcal{H}_{b}^{\nu}\subset(\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda})^{\perp}\subset\mathcal{H}^{<\mu}

that define the action of the generator
e\in{\rm Cl}_{-1}
. The inclusions
g_{\lambda\mu}
and odd endomorphisms
e^{\lambda\mu}
satisfy a compatibility property on triple intersections. By Corollary
C.13
, we obtain a map to the
n
th space in the
{\rm KO}
-spectrum (
287
).
A refinement of open covers associated with
\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
, determines a continuous functor between the associated Čech categories that after realization is a homotopy equivalence. This leads to a pair of functors (
C.13
) and restriction of the operators (
289
) furnish a natural isomorphism between these functors. By
[
Seg68
, Proposition 2.1]
, the resulting maps
{\rm Ind}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{A}}),{\rm Ind}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}({\mathbb{A}})\colon M\to{\rm KO}^{-n}
are homotopic.
∎

The connections
\nabla^{<\lambda}
on
\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}
determine a Čech–de Rham cocycle representing the Pontryagin character of the class associated with (
287
) that we denote by

(290)

\displaystyle{\rm Ch}({\rm Ind}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{A}}))=[U_{\lambda},{\rm Ch}(\nabla^{<\lambda})]\in C^{*}(\{U_{\lambda}\},\Omega^{\bullet}).

Lemma C.18
.

Let
{\mathbb{A}}
be a self-adjoint Clifford linear superconnection that admits smooth index bundles. The differential forms (
282
) witness a coboundary in the Čech–de Rham complex between (
290
) and the (globally defined) cocycle
{\rm Ch}({\mathbb{A}})
. Hence,

(291)

\displaystyle\widehat{{\rm Ind}}_{\Lambda}({\mathbb{A}})=[\{\mathcal{H}^{<\lambda}\to U_{\lambda},\nabla^{<\lambda},\eta_{\lambda},g_{\lambda\mu},e^{\lambda\mu}\}]\in\widehat{\rm KO}{}^{n}(M)

exacts a differential cocycle from the superconnection
{\mathbb{A}}
.

Proof.

The Čech–de Rham coboundary follows from (
283
). The differential cocycle (
291
) uses the description of differential
{\rm KO}
-theory from
[
BE23b
, §7]
.
∎

The Bismut superconnection is the main example of a superconnection that admits smooth index bundles, as we review below.

Definition C.19
.

A
spin structure
for a rank
n
real, oriented, metrized vector bundle
V\to M
is a
{\rm Spin}(n)
-principal bundle
P_{V}\to M
equipped with a double cover
P_{V}\to{\rm Fr}(V)
of the oriented frame bundle of
V
that is equivariant relative to
{\rm Spin}(n)\to{\rm SO}(n)
.

The collection of spin structures on
V
forms a category (possibly empty) whose morphisms are isomorphisms of principal bundles compatible with the map of the frame bundle of
V
. For a family
\pi\colon X\to M
of Riemannian manifolds, a spin structure on the family is a spin structure on the vertical tangent bundle
T(X/M)=\ker(d\pi)\subset TM
. We will require two types of bundles of Clifford modules built out of a spin structure.

Definition C.20
.

For
\pi\colon X\to M
a bundle of Riemannian manifolds for which
T(X/M)
has a spin structure, the
spinor bundle
is

(292)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{S}}_{X/M}=P_{T(X/M)}\times_{{\rm Spin}(n)}{\rm Cl}_{n}.

The sections of
{\mathbb{S}}_{X/M}
support an
M
-family of Dirac operators
\not{D}
that graded commute with the left
{\rm Cl}_{-n}={\rm Cl}_{n}^{\sf{op}}
action.

Using the work of Quillen
[
Qui85
]
and Bismut
[
Bis86
]
, the index bundle data (
280
), (
288
), and (
289
) may be refined to a differential KO-class. There is a unitary
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-linear connection
\nabla^{\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\otimes V)}
on
\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\otimes V)
, constructed from the Levi-Civita connection on
{\mathbb{S}}
, the connection on
V
, and the mean curvature of the fibers of
\pi\colon X\to M

[
BGV92
, Proposition 9.13]
. The
Bismut superconnection
is defined as
[
BGV92
, Proposition 10.15]

(293)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{A}}=\not{D}\otimes V+\nabla^{\pi_{*}({\mathbb{S}}\otimes V)}-\frac{c(T)}{4}

where
c(T)
is Clifford multiplication by the curvature 2-form
T
of the bundle
\pi\colon X\to M
. This incarnation of the Bismut superconnection is real and
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-linear: each of the three terms on the right of (
293
) independently commutes with the
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-action (the operator
c(T)
uses the
{\rm Cl}(T(X/M))
-action, which commutes with the
{\rm Cl}_{-n}
-action).

Proposition C.21
.

Any finite-rank self-adjoint Clifford linear superconnnection admits cutoffs. Self-adjoint Clifford linear superconnections adapted to a family of Clifford linear Dirac operators admit cutoffs.

Proof.

The finite rank case is
[
BGV92
, Theorem 9.7]
. For superconnections adapted to Dirac operators (without Clifford actions), this follows from
[
BGV92
, Proposition 9.10 and Theorem 9.26]
, see also
[
FL10
, §7.2]
. The straightforward extension of these results to Clifford linear Dirac operators is
[
BE23b
, §8.2-8.3]
.
∎
